<browser_instruction># Claude in Chrome browser automation

You have access to browser automation tools (mcp__claude-in-chrome__*) for interacting with web pages in Chrome. Follow these guidelines for effective browser automation.

## Loading deferred tools

If the mcp__claude-in-chrome__* tools are deferred (must be loaded via ToolSearch before use), load every tool you expect to need in ONE ToolSearch call — the select query accepts a comma-separated list — never one call per tool. Start with the core set:

ToolSearch with query "select:mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_context_mcp,mcp__claude-in-chrome__navigate,mcp__claude-in-chrome__computer,mcp__claude-in-chrome__read_page,mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_create_mcp,mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_close_mcp"

Add task-specific tools to the same call when the task obviously needs them: read_console_messages / read_network_requests for debugging, form_input for forms, gif_creator for recordings, javascript_tool for page scripting.

## GIF recording

When performing multi-step browser interactions that the user may want to review or share, use mcp__claude-in-chrome__gif_creator to record them.

You must ALWAYS:
* Capture extra frames before and after taking actions to ensure smooth playback
* Name the file meaningfully to help the user identify it later (e.g., "login_process.gif")

## Console log debugging

You can use mcp__claude-in-chrome__read_console_messages to read console output. Console output may be verbose. If you are looking for specific log entries, use the 'pattern' parameter with a regex-compatible pattern. This filters results efficiently and avoids overwhelming output. For example, use pattern: "[MyApp]" to filter for application-specific logs rather than reading all console output.

## Alerts and dialogs

IMPORTANT: Do not trigger JavaScript alerts, confirms, prompts, or browser modal dialogs through your actions. These browser dialogs block all further browser events and will prevent the extension from receiving any subsequent commands. Instead, when possible, use console.log for debugging and then use the mcp__claude-in-chrome__read_console_messages tool to read those log messages. If a page has dialog-triggering elements:
1. Avoid clicking buttons or links that may trigger alerts (e.g., "Delete" buttons with confirmation dialogs)
2. If you must interact with such elements, warn the user first that this may interrupt the session
3. Use mcp__claude-in-chrome__javascript_tool to check for and dismiss any existing dialogs before proceeding

If you accidentally trigger a dialog and lose responsiveness, inform the user they need to manually dismiss it in the browser.

## Avoid rabbit holes and loops

When using browser automation tools, stay focused on the specific task. If you encounter any of the following, stop and ask the user for guidance:
- Unexpected complexity or tangential browser exploration
- Browser tool calls failing or returning errors after 2-3 attempts
- No response from the browser extension
- Page elements not responding to clicks or input
- Pages not loading or timing out
- Unable to complete the browser task despite multiple approaches

Explain what you attempted, what went wrong, and ask how the user would like to proceed. Do not keep retrying the same failing browser action or explore unrelated pages without checking in first.

## Tab context and session startup

IMPORTANT: At the start of each browser automation session, call mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_context_mcp first to get information about the user's current browser tabs. Use this context to understand what the user might want to work with before creating new tabs.

Never reuse tab IDs from a previous/other session. Follow these guidelines:
1. Only reuse an existing tab if the user explicitly asks to work with it
2. Otherwise, create a new tab with mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_create_mcp
3. If a tool returns an error indicating the tab doesn't exist or is invalid, call tabs_context_mcp to get fresh tab IDs
4. When a tab is closed by the user or a navigation error occurs, call tabs_context_mcp to see what tabs are available</browser_instruction>

1	# explainroo for coding agents
2	
3	This file tells you, the coding agent, how to make a video with explainroo.
4	The user usually gives you a topic, and maybe a length or a style. You write
5	the words and the pictures. explainroo makes the video.
6	
7	explainroo turns two files into an MP4 video with narration. `script.md`
8	holds what the voice says. `scenes.js` draws what the viewer sees. Every
9	drawing can appear on a word the voice says. A voice model (Kokoro) reads
10	the script on this computer, or the narration comes from ElevenLabs or your
11	own recording. Whisper listens to the narration and writes down when each
12	word is spoken. Chrome draws the frames in the background.
13	explainroo adds music and sound effects, and ffmpeg makes the MP4. You need
14	no API key for local voices or recordings. ElevenLabs and AI images need
15	keys.
16	
17	The full documentation for people is at https://www.explainroo.com/docs/.
18	
19	## Before you start
20	
21	Maybe the user pasted the prompt from the README or from explainroo.com, and
22	you are not in an explainroo folder yet. Then clone it into the current
23	folder first. If the user named another place, use that.
24	
25	```bash
26	git clone https://github.com/vincentsch/explainroo.git
27	cd explainroo
28	```
29	
30	Run this once in the explainroo folder:
31	
32	```bash
33	npm install
34	node bin/explainroo.js doctor --fetch
35	```
36	
37	`doctor` checks Node, ffmpeg and Chrome, and the extra programs some
38	languages need. It also downloads the English speech models, about 400 MB,
39	the first time only. Other languages fetch their speech check model on first
40	use. If the user ran `npm link`, you
41	can write `explainroo` instead of `node bin/explainroo.js` in the commands
42	below. Commands that work on a video take the project folder first, and every
43	command accepts `--json`.
44	
45	### When to ask the user
46	
47	Decide the normal things yourself, like the look, the length, the voice, the
48	layout and the wording. Ask only when you cannot go on without an answer.
49	When you ask, put everything you need in one message. Typical reasons to ask:
50	
51	- The topic is unclear, or you need a fact you cannot check.
52	- The user wants AI images and there is no OpenRouter key (see Images).
53	- The user wants ElevenLabs and there is no ElevenLabs key (see Voices).
54	- The user wants something explainroo cannot do, like video shot with a
55	  camera.
56	
57	## Making a video, step by step
58	
59	1. **Learn the subject.** Read the code, docs or pages the user points to.
60	   Write down the facts you will use. Never make up numbers, quotes or claims.
61	   If a number matters and you cannot check it, leave it out.
62	
63	2. **Create the project.**
64	
65	   ```bash
66	   node bin/explainroo.js init videos/<name> --theme paper --title "..."
67	   ```
68	
69	   Projects go in `videos/`. Git ignores that folder. Pick the look that fits
70	   the audience: `paper` (friendly, hand drawn, the default), `clean`
71	   (products and business), `chalk` (teaching), `blueprint` (engineering),
72	   `midnight` (developer tools) or `editorial` (essays, history, money and
73	   other thoughtful topics). Pick the size for the place the video goes,
74	   for example `--size tiktok` or `--size linkedin`. The sizes are listed in
75	   "Sizes for each platform" below. Add `--pace 1.2` when the user wants a
76	   quicker video.
77	
78	3. **Write `script.md`.** The narration comes first. It sets the timing for
79	   everything else. See "How to write the narration" below.
80	
81	4. **Make the voice.**
82	
83	   ```bash
84	   node bin/explainroo.js voice videos/<name>
85	   ```
86	
87	   The command prints how long each scene is and how many words the speech
88	   check confirmed. If a word is not confirmed, the voice probably said it
89	   wrong. Fix it with `{shown|spoken}` in the script and run the command
90	   again. For a recording, make script.md say what was actually said.
91	   Only the scenes you changed are made again. `check`, `still`, `sheet`,
92	   `render` and `preview` also make missing narration, so start them after
93	   `voice` has finished, not at the same time.
94	
95	5. **Plan the pictures.** For each scene, decide what is on screen when each
96	   marker or important word is spoken. Decide where each thing sits and what
97	   leaves the screen. Keep things in the same place from scene to scene.
98	   Answer four questions for each scene. What is the one thing the viewer
99	   should look at? Which relationship does the layout show: an order, a
100	   comparison, the parts of a whole or a cause? What appears and what leaves
101	   on each cue? What stays in the same place in the next scene? "Layouts that
102	   work" below has four layouts to start from.
103	
104	6. **Make images, if the video needs them.** Icons and diagrams cover most
105	   technical topics. Everyday how-to topics like cooking, cars or gardening
106	   work better with pictures. See "Images" below.
107	
108	7. **Write `scenes.js`.** Write one function per scene. Tie every picture to
109	   the narration with `at: 'word'` or `at: '#marker'`. The scene API is at
110	   the end of this file.
111	
112	8. **Check.**
113	
114	   ```bash
115	   node bin/explainroo.js check videos/<name>
116	   ```
117	
118	   Fix every error and every warning. You decide what to do with the hints.
119	
120	9. **Look at the frames.** You cannot watch the video, so this is how you see
121	   it.
122	
123	   ```bash
124	   node bin/explainroo.js still videos/<name>                 # the end of each scene
125	   node bin/explainroo.js still videos/<name> intro@2.5 12.0  # any moment
126	   node bin/explainroo.js sheet videos/<name> --scene intro   # one scene over time
127	   node bin/explainroo.js sheet videos/<name>                 # the whole video
128	   ```
129	
130	   Open the images and judge them like a viewer would. Is the text big
131	   enough? Is anything cut off or on top of something else? Is half the frame
132	   empty? Does the sheet show something new every few seconds? Fix what is
133	   wrong and look again.
134	
135	   For Reddit and other feeds, also run `check videos/<name> --view-width
136	   854`. It warns when visible text shrinks below 16px in that player.
137	   This is a size check, not a guarantee about platform compression. Show
138	   only the current query or result, use bigger type, and zoom into details.
139	   Review a still at `--scale 0.4448` too (about 480p for a 1080p video).
140	   Never upload `draft.mp4`. After uploading, check the platform's lower
141	   quality copy as well as its full-size copy.
142	
143	10. **Make the video and check the file.**
144	
145	    ```bash
146	    node bin/explainroo.js render videos/<name> --draft   # quick half-size version
147	    node bin/explainroo.js render videos/<name>           # final video
148	    node bin/explainroo.js verify videos/<name>
149	    ```
150	
151	    `render` also writes subtitle files (`.srt` and `.vtt`) and a cover
152	    image (`.cover.jpg`) next to the video. When scenes name chapters, it
153	    writes `.chapters.txt` for the YouTube description and adds the chapters
154	    to the MP4. `--format gif` makes a silent GIF instead, for a README or a
155	    post. `verify` checks the MP4. It measures the loudness and looks for
156	    black frames and silence. It also runs the speech check on the finished
157	    sound to make sure the voice is still clear over the music.
158	
159	11. **Tell the user** where the video and its subtitle files, chapters and
160	    cover are, how long the video is, what verify said, and what you could
161	    not check.
162	
163	## How to write the narration
164	
165	Unless the user asks for another style, write the way a person explains
166	something to a friend at a table.
167	
168	- Use regular, down-to-earth English and everyday words. Explain things in
169	  simple terms. If you need a technical word, say what it means the first
170	  time.
171	- Keep sentences short, about 8 to 18 words, one idea each.
172	- Say who does what: "The browser asks a resolver."
173	- Every sentence adds a fact. Cut half sentences that repeat something.
174	- Do not use em dashes or dashes between phrases. Use a comma or a new
175	  sentence.
176	- Leave out slogans and punchlines ("Just code."), "not X, but Y" twists,
177	  colon reveals, chains of three short sentences, metaphors, and filler words
178	  like "just", "simply", "really", "actually" and "exactly".
179	- Do not tell the viewer to do obvious things ("Let's dive in", "Stay tuned").
180	- End with a plain sentence that sums up the main point.
181	
182	Length and pace:
183	
184	- Aim for 45 to 120 seconds unless the user says otherwise. A finished video
185	  has about 150 words a minute, pauses included. In Japanese it is about 270
186	  characters a minute, and in Chinese about 200.
187	- Start with the question or problem. Explain it in small steps. Give one
188	  concrete example, then sum up.
189	- One idea per scene, usually 1 to 3 sentences (4 to 15 seconds).
190	- Lists read aloud ("A, B, C and D") only work when each item appears on
191	  screen.
192	
193	Script syntax:
194	
195	```markdown
196	# Title of the video
197	
198	## scene-id {hold=1.5}
199	Narration for this scene. [#marker] A marker names a moment you can animate on.
200	[pause 0.6] adds silence. {SQL|sequel} shows "SQL" in captions but says "sequel".
201	> Lines starting with > are notes for you and are not spoken.
202	```
203	
204	- `## scene-id` starts a scene. The id must match a function in `scenes.js`.
205	- Put a `[#marker]` wherever a picture should change. Every sentence should
206	  trigger at least one picture.
207	- Use `{shown|spoken}` for anything the voice may misread: acronyms
208	  (`{CLI|C L I}`), versions (`{v2.1|version two point one}`), symbols and
209	  domains (`{example.com|example dot com}`, otherwise the voice says "example
210	  comm").
211	- A scene can have these settings in the braces after its id: `hold`
212	  (seconds after the voice, default 0.7), `min` (the shortest the scene can
213	  be, in seconds), `lead` (seconds before the voice, default 0.35) and
214	  `transition` (`fade`, `slide`, `wipe`, `zoom`, `brush`, `cut`).
215	- A scene without narration lasts `min` seconds (default 3).
216	- `chapter="How the resolver finds it"` in a scene's braces starts a
217	  chapter at that scene. It lasts until the next chapter. Titles have 1 to
218	  60 characters. If the first scene names no chapter, the first chapter is
219	  the video title. YouTube shows chapters only when there are at least 3
220	  and each lasts 10 seconds or more.
221	
222	## What makes the pictures good
223	
224	- **Show it when it is said.** A picture appears as the voice mentions it.
225	  Use word cues.
226	- **Something changes every 2 to 4 seconds.** A new element, an arrow or a
227	  highlight. The camera adds its own moves. `check` points out long stretches
228	  where nothing new appears.
229	- **Short screen text.** Labels, numbers and key phrases of a few words. The
230	  whole narration never goes on screen. When the narration should be readable,
231	  turn on captions (`"captions": true`). Then do not also show the sentence
232	  the voice is saying as text.
233	- **Few things at once.** At most five or six elements. Take things off the
234	  screen with `out` before the next idea.
235	- **Big and readable.** Titles 80 to 110 px, labels 40 to 56 px, small text
236	  at least 32 px on a 1920 x 1080 canvas. Stay inside `s.safe`.
237	- **Same meaning, same color.** Give each thing one color and one place and
238	  keep them. Use the accent color for the one thing that matters most.
239	- **Real examples.** A command you can run, a number with its source or a
240	  picture of the real screen is better than three boxes with general words.
241	- **A clear last frame** that shows the main point.
242	
243	### Layouts that work
244	
245	These four layouts cover many scenes: one big number, a comparison, steps in
246	order, and one main thing with its parts. Start from the one that fits and
247	change the words, colors and cues. Each one also works in tall and square
248	frames.
249	
250	```js
251	export default {
252	  // One big number with a short caption under it.
253	  number(s) {
254	    const u = Math.max(s.W, s.H) / 100;
255	    // The number also has to fit across a narrow, tall frame.
256	    const n = s.number(1000000, { y: s.cy - u * 2, size: Math.min(u * 10, s.safe.w / 6), at: '#number' });
257	    s.text('requests a day', { y: n.bottom + u * 3, size: u * 3.6, color: 'muted', at: '#caption' });
258	  },
259	
260	  // Two things side by side, or stacked in a tall frame.
261	  compare(s) {
262	    const u = Math.max(s.W, s.H) / 100;
263	    const cells = s.grid(s.portrait ? 1 : 2, s.portrait ? 2 : 1, { gap: u * 4 });
264	    const sides = [
265	      { title: 'Callbacks', note: 'one function inside another', color: 'orange', at: '#left' },
266	      { title: 'async / await', note: 'reads from top to bottom', color: 'green', at: '#right' },
267	    ];
268	    sides.forEach(({ title, note, color, at }, i) => {
269	      const c = cells[i];
270	      // The note hangs from the card, so a note that wraps grows downward.
271	      const card = s.box(title, { x: c.x, y: c.y - u * 3, w: c.w * 0.8, size: u * 4, color, at });
272	      s.text(note, { x: c.x, y: card.bottom + u * 1.2, valign: 'top', size: u * 2.6, color: 'muted', maxWidth: c.w * 0.85, at });
273	    });
274	  },
275	
276	  // Steps in order, left to right, or top to bottom in a tall frame.
277	  steps(s) {
278	    const u = Math.max(s.W, s.H) / 100;
279	    const labels = ['Browser', 'Resolver', 'Address'];
280	    const xs = s.portrait ? labels.map(() => s.cx) : s.row(labels.length, { width: s.safe.w * 0.72 });
281	    const ys = s.portrait ? s.col(labels.length) : labels.map(() => s.cy);
282	    labels.forEach((label, i) => {
283	      const at = i ? `#step${i + 1}` : 0.3;
284	      s.box(label, { id: `step${i}`, x: xs[i], y: ys[i], size: u * 3.6, at });
285	      if (i) s.arrow(`step${i - 1}`, `step${i}`, { at });
286	    });
287	  },
288	
289	  // One main thing with its parts around it. A tall frame has no room
290	  // beside it, so the parts sit in a row underneath.
291	  hub(s) {
292	    const u = Math.max(s.W, s.H) / 100;
293	    const parts = ['Email', 'Calendar', 'Files'];
294	    const hubY = s.portrait ? s.cy - s.safe.h * 0.18 : s.cy;
295	    const spots = s.portrait
296	      ? s.row(parts.length, { width: s.safe.w * 0.72 }).map((x) => ({ x, y: s.cy + s.safe.h * 0.16 }))
297	      : [-90, 30, 150].map((d) => ({ x: s.cx + Math.cos((d * Math.PI) / 180) * s.safe.w * 0.34, y: s.cy + Math.sin((d * Math.PI) / 180) * s.safe.h * 0.3 }));
298	    s.box('Login server', { id: 'hub', x: s.cx, y: hubY, size: u * 4, color: 'accent', at: '#hub' });
299	    parts.forEach((label, i) => {
300	      const at = `#spoke${i + 1}`;
301	      s.box(label, { id: `part${i}`, ...spots[i], size: u * (s.portrait ? 2.4 : 3.2), at });
302	      s.arrow('hub', `part${i}`, { at });
303	    });
304	  },
305	};
306	```
307	
308	Text sizes come from the frame's long side, so they look about the same in
309	wide and tall frames. A big number also has to fit across a narrow frame.
310	
311	### The camera
312	
313	explainroo films each scene with a camera that follows the narration. It
314	opens on the picture. When a scene starts small, the camera starts close and
315	widens as new things need room. Something new that already fits the shot
316	needs no move. Now and then the camera moves in on what the voice talks
317	about, for example a new card and the one it relates to, when the viewer has
318	time to look. Then it follows new things at that distance. Near the end of
319	the scene it pulls back, so the last frame shows the whole idea. During each
320	scene it also pushes in very slowly.
321	
322	While a shot holds, every card and label is either fully in the shot or out
323	of it. Arrows and lines may run off the edge, but their labels stay whole, and
324	so do the labels of charts. While the camera moves from
325	one shot to the next, things may pass the edge of the frame for a moment.
326	Shots stay clear of the captions and of the parts the app covers on Shorts,
327	TikTok and Reels.
328	
329	Lay out each scene as one picture on the full frame, the way it should look
330	at the end, and let the camera find the close-ups:
331	
332	- Put the next element near the one it follows, so one shot shows both.
333	- A scene that starts with one thing and grows gets the most camera moves.
334	  The camera starts close and widens with the picture.
335	- Keep a box with its label, or an icon with its caption, together as one
336	  unit. Things far apart get their own shots.
337	- Lines, arrows and things added inside an element already on screen, like
338	  a line in a chart, get no close-up of their own. Neither does an icon
339	  without a label or a box without words, because up close it shows nothing
340	  new. Give it a label, or use `s.focus` when one should get a close-up.
341	- When a scene goes on with the same picture, draw the carried elements with
342	  `at: -1` in the same places. The camera then goes on from where it
343	  stopped, and the two scenes fade into each other.
344	
345	The camera measures every element, including what `s.draw` draws, and
346	follows the `at` and `out` times. It measures each element where it is at
347	the end of the scene. When something travels across the frame during a
348	scene, keep that scene's camera still with `s.camera([])`. Anything drawn straight on `s.ctx` counts
349	as one block that is there for the whole scene, so the camera keeps it whole
350	and cannot film it on its own. Use `s.draw` for a drawing that appears on a
351	cue. Highlighted code lines and terminal commands get close-ups of their own
352	when they are small in the shot. `s.focus` points the camera at any part of
353	the picture.
354	
355	`s.camera([...])` in a scene takes over that scene's camera, and
356	`s.camera([])` keeps the scene still. `"camera": "still"` in `video.json`
357	turns the camera off for the whole video. `check` checks the layout as you
358	wrote it, without the camera. Stills, sheets and renders show the camera.
359	
360	The final render adds motion blur to frames where something moves, like a
361	film camera. Drafts skip it. `"motionBlur": false` turns it off.
362	
363	## Voices
364	
365	Kokoro runs on this computer and needs no key. Pick a voice with
366	`explainroo voices`. A string in `video.json` keeps using Kokoro:
367	
368	```json
369	"voice": "af_heart"
370	```
371	
372	`{ "provider": "kokoro", "id": "af_heart" }` means the same thing.
373	
374	ElevenLabs offers premium and cloned voices. It costs money on the user's
375	account and receives the script text. If the user wants it and there is no
376	key, ask them for an API key from elevenlabs.io. Set `ELEVENLABS_API_KEY` in
377	the environment, or save `ELEVENLABS_API_KEY=...` in a `.env` file in the
378	project folder or the explainroo folder. The environment takes priority,
379	then the project file. Never print the key, and never put it in a script,
380	a commit or a log. Keep `.env` out of git.
381	
382	```bash
383	node bin/explainroo.js voices videos/<name> --elevenlabs
384	node bin/explainroo.js voices videos/<name> --elevenlabs --json
385	```
386	
387	The list shows the names, ids and labels available to that key. Use an id
388	in `video.json`:
389	
390	```json
391	"voice": { "provider": "elevenlabs", "id": "JBFqnCBsd6RMkjVDRZzb", "model": "eleven_multilingual_v2" }
392	```
393	
394	`model` is optional and defaults to `eleven_multilingual_v2`. The voice
395	command reports the characters used. Each request is saved in
396	`build/voice/elevenlabs-usage.jsonl`, without the key or script text.
397	ElevenLabs uses `speed` times `pace`, limited to 0.7 through 1.2.
398	The voice command tells you when it uses that limit.
399	
400	You can also record the narration yourself. Use a quiet room and read
401	script.md as written. Put one take for the whole video in `assets/`:
402	
403	```json
404	"voice": { "provider": "recording", "src": "assets/narration.m4a" }
405	```
406	
407	Or put one file per scene in a folder:
408	
409	```json
410	"voice": { "provider": "recording", "src": "assets/narration/" }
411	```
412	
413	Each scene with narration needs `<scene-id>.<ext>`, for example `intro.wav`.
414	Supported formats are wav, mp3, m4a, aac, flac, ogg and opus. Files must stay
415	inside `assets/`, with no `..` in the path.
416	
417	One take is split into scenes using the words in script.md. Each cut keeps
418	about 0.15 seconds around the speech. Pauses within a scene stay as recorded.
419	The scene's `lead` and `hold` add the gaps between scenes. Recordings are
420	made mono and their volume is brought close to Kokoro's. Their sample rate
421	is kept up to 48 kHz. Changing `pace` or an explicit `speed` changes their
422	tempo without changing pitch.
423	
424	`[pause]`, `sentenceGap` and `paragraphGap` do not add silence to recordings.
425	Markers `[#name]`, captions, cues and `{shown|spoken}` still work. If the
426	speech check reports a mismatch, make script.md say what was actually said.
427	Replacing a scene recording makes that scene again. Replacing a whole take
428	makes all its scenes again.
429	
430	ElevenLabs narration and recordings work in every supported language.
431	Run `voice`, `check` and `render` as usual with any of the three sources.
432	
433	## Videos in other languages
434	
435	Set `language` in `video.json` to `en`, `es`, `fr`, `it`, `pt-br`, `hi`,
436	`ja` or `zh`. English is the default. `pt-br` means Brazilian Portuguese and
437	`zh` Mandarin Chinese. You can also start with
438	`explainroo init videos/my-video --language es`.
439	
440	When `voice` is left out, the language picks its Kokoro voice:
441	
442	| Language | Default voice |
443	|---|---|
444	| English (`en`) | `af_heart` |
445	| Spanish (`es`) | `ef_dora` |
446	| French (`fr`) | `ff_siwis` |
447	| Italian (`it`) | `if_sara` |
448	| Brazilian Portuguese (`pt-br`) | `pf_dora` |
449	| Hindi (`hi`) | `hf_alpha` |
450	| Japanese (`ja`) | `jf_alpha` |
451	| Mandarin Chinese (`zh`) | `zf_xiaobei` |
452	
453	Run `explainroo voices --language es` to see voices for one language.
454	A Kokoro voice must match the video's language. If a project already has
455	`voice` set, change it too, or remove it to use the default.
456	
457	Write the narration in that language as a person would say it. Do not
458	translate English word for word. The writing rules above apply in spirit.
459	Cue words and `[#markers]` work the same. `at: 'qué'` matches "Qué".
460	Hindi sentences can end with `।`. Captions and subtitle files keep the
461	written text. The looks include a Hindi font, so no system font is needed.
462	
463	Spanish, French, Italian, Portuguese and Hindi need the espeak-ng program.
464	Install it on Linux
465	with `sudo apt install espeak-ng`, or on macOS with `brew install espeak-ng`.
466	On Windows, use the installer from the
467	[espeak-ng releases page](https://github.com/espeak-ng/espeak-ng/releases).
468	If the program is in another place, set `EXPLAINROO_ESPEAK` to its binary.
469	English, Japanese, Chinese, ElevenLabs and recordings do not need it.
470	
471	### Japanese and Chinese
472	
473	Japanese and Chinese need Python 3 with misaki, the pronunciation library
474	Kokoro's voices were trained with. Install what the language needs:
475	
476	```bash
477	python3 -m pip install "misaki[ja]"   # Japanese
478	python3 -m unidic download            # Japanese dictionary, about 526 MB
479	python3 -m pip install "misaki[zh]"   # Chinese
480	```
481	
482	On Windows, write `python` instead of `python3`. If misaki is in a virtual
483	environment, set `EXPLAINROO_PYTHON` to that environment's Python.
484	ElevenLabs and recordings need neither.
485	
486	Write the narration as usual, without spaces between words. explainroo
487	finds the words itself, so cue words and markers work: `at: '解析'` matches
488	the word 解析, and a phrase like `at: '名前の解析'` works too. A cue must
489	match whole words. Put a `[#marker]` where you need a moment inside a word.
490	Sentences end with `。`, `！` or `？`.
491	
492	Latin words such as API or JavaScript are read in English. For a Japanese
493	reading, write it out: `{API|エーピーアイ}`. Numbers can stay in digits,
494	including decimals like 365.2422. The Japanese reader sometimes picks the
495	wrong reading for a word, such as ついたち for 一日 (write `1日` for
496	いちにち) or こよみ for 暦 in グレゴリオ暦 (write `{グレゴリオ暦|グレゴリオれき}`).
497	When the speech check flags a word, compare what it heard with the script
498	before you change anything.
499	
500	Whisper often writes a word with other characters that sound the same, so
501	the speech check compares sounds. Japanese is compared by its kana reading,
502	so 世紀 heard as 正規 still counts. Chinese is compared by pinyin without
503	tones, and Traditional and Simplified characters count as the same. So do
504	not reword a sentence only because Whisper chose other characters. Captions
505	and subtitles join words without spaces, with up to 18 characters per
506	subtitle line, and never break inside a word. Keep the shown text of an
507	override short.
508	
509	The looks' fonts have no Japanese or Chinese characters. The first `voice`,
510	`still` or `render` downloads Noto Sans JP or Noto Sans SC, regular and bold,
511	about 33 MB, and checks the files. They are saved in
512	`~/.cache/explainroo/fonts/` (or `$EXPLAINROO_CACHE/fonts/`) and used in
513	every look, in captions and in app screens. After that first download,
514	`EXPLAINROO_OFFLINE=1` works. A web player made with `export` keeps only
515	the characters the video shows, so its fonts stay small.
516	
517	The first speech check in another language downloads multilingual Whisper
518	small. `doctor --fetch` still fetches only the English models. `verify`
519	uses the video's language to check the finished sound too.
520	
521	If a symbol, short form or word sounds wrong, use `{shown|spoken}` in
522	`script.md` and run `voice` again. Put the pronunciation in the same
523	language. The speech check accepts digits for numbers. It does not try to
524	match spelled numbers to digits outside English. Write the digits, or use
525	an override whose spoken text has digits when that helps the check.
526	
527	In Hindi, the speech check confirms fewer words than in the other languages,
528	because the speech model knows Hindi less well. A few unconfirmed words are
529	normal there. Fix a word when it stays unconfirmed in several scenes, or
530	when the user hears it wrong.
531	
532	## Images
533	
534	explainroo can make illustrations with AI image models through OpenRouter.
535	This is optional. It costs money on the user's OpenRouter account, roughly 7
536	to 13 cents per image at the time of writing.
537	
538	If the user wants images and there is no key, ask them for an OpenRouter API
539	key (from openrouter.ai/keys). Save it in a `.env` file in the explainroo
540	folder as `OPENROUTER_API_KEY=...`, or set it as an environment variable. Git
541	ignores `.env`. Never print the key, and never put it in a script, a commit
542	or a log.
543	
544	```bash
545	node bin/explainroo.js image videos/<name> cables "Two cars parked nose to nose with their hoods open, jumper cables between the batteries"
546	node bin/explainroo.js images videos/<name>    # what was made and what it cost
547	```
548	
549	This saves `assets/cables.png`. Use it in a scene with
550	`s.image('assets/cables.png', { w: 1100, frame: 'card', at: 'cables' })`.
551	
552	- `--model best` (default) uses OpenAI GPT Image 2. It does what you ask and
553	  is worth the price for most images.
554	- `--model cheap` uses Google Gemini 3.1 Flash Image. It costs about half,
555	  but it often ignores parts of the prompt.
556	- `--aspect` sets the shape of the image: `16:9` (default for landscape
557	  videos), `9:16`, `1:1`, `4:3`, `3:2` and their portrait forms.
558	- `--ref assets/a.png` sends an earlier image along, so a person or object
559	  keeps the same look. Separate several files with commas.
560	- Every image gets a default style (flat, soft colors, no text). Set your own
561	  in `video.json` with `"images": { "style": "...", "model": "best" }`, or
562	  turn the style off with `--no-style`.
563	
564	**Always open every image and check it.** Image models get details wrong:
565	extra fingers, cables on the wrong terminal, text that looks like writing but
566	is not. If anything is wrong, change the prompt and make the image again.
567	Keep text out of images. Put words on screen with `s.text` instead. Use
568	images for things icons cannot show, and keep one style through the whole
569	video.
570	
571	## Product demos
572	
573	A product demo is the kind of video software companies make to show their
574	app. You rebuild the app's screens with `s.ui`, which has cards, fields,
575	buttons, dropdowns, toggles and status labels in the app's colors and fonts.
576	A mouse pointer clicks through the screens and types into the fields, and the
577	camera zooms in on what the voice talks about. `examples/unspar-demo` is a
578	complete one.
579	
580	**Capture the live site first.**
581	
582	```bash
583	node bin/explainroo.js capture videos/<name> https://example.com --mobile
584	```
585	
586	`capture` opens the page in a fresh browser, without cookies or logins, and
587	saves what a demo needs in `assets/capture/<hostname>/`:
588	
589	- `page.png` (the first screen) and `full.png` (the whole page, up to 8000
590	  pixels tall), at twice the pixel density, so they stay sharp when the
591	  camera zooms in. `--mobile` adds `mobile.png` and `mobile-full.png`.
592	- `capture.json` with the colors, fonts, logo candidates and the page's
593	  wording: headings, navigation, buttons and form labels. Copy the wording
594	  exactly. The live site is the source of truth for claims and wording.
595	- Up to three logo files, and the font files that come from Google Fonts, in
596	  the weights the page uses.
597	
598	It prints a `brand` and `fonts` block to paste into `video.json`. Check it
599	against the screenshots. Fonts built into explainroo need no files. For other
600	fonts, get the files, check the license and copy the license file next to
601	them. Cookie banners and chat widgets can appear in the screenshots, as they
602	do for any visitor. Use a screenshot, or a part of one, without them.
603	Capturing the same site again replaces its folder.
604	
605	You can also show a real screen recording with `s.clip` in a `browser`
606	frame. Use `s.camera` to zoom in on the detail the voice mentions.
607	
608	**Keep it true.** A demo is advertising, so everything in it has to be right.
609	
610	- Take the wording from the app itself: its templates and language files
611	  (for example `lang/en/*.php`, `locales/`, or the components). Copy labels,
612	  buttons and status names exactly.
613	- Show only features that exist and that customers can use. Leave out admin
614	  screens, debug views and plans that are not built yet.
615	- Example data must look like example data: `example.com`, a made up project,
616	  a made up article title. Never invent customers, numbers or results.
617	- Claims in the narration come from the product's live website. Show prices
618	  only if the website shows them.
619	- Ask the user if the website and the app say different things.
620	
621	**Set up the brand** in `video.json`. Use the `clean` look.
622	
623	```json
624	{
625	  "theme": "clean",
626	  "brand": {
627	    "accent": "#245cff",
628	    "background": "#f8f6f1",
629	    "font": "Figtree",
630	    "headline": "Instrument Serif",
631	    "logo": "assets/logo.svg"
632	  },
633	  "fonts": [
634	    { "family": "Figtree", "src": "assets/fonts/Figtree-600.woff2", "weight": 600 }
635	  ]
636	}
637	```
638	
639	- `accent` is the main button color. Find it in the app's Tailwind config or
640	  CSS. `background` is the color behind the screens, `ink` the color of text
641	  drawn right on it.
642	- `font` is the app's font. Inter is built in; other fonts go in
643	  `assets/fonts/` and in `fonts` (`.woff2`, `.woff`, `.ttf` or `.otf`, one
644	  entry per weight, or `"weight": "100 900"` for a variable font, and
645	  `"style": "italic"`). Copy the font's license file next to it.
646	- `headline` is the font for big headlines. Instrument Serif is built in. If
647	  the brand has no serif, use its own font and pass `{ weight: 700,
648	  italic: false }` to `u.headline()`.
649	- `logo` is the product's logo from the app or website, best as SVG.
650	
651	**Build a scene** in this order: background, camera, headline, screens,
652	cursor last so it stays on top.
653	
654	```js
655	hook(s) {
656	  const u = s.ui;
657	  u.backdrop();
658	  u.floaters();
659	  s.camera([{ at: '#site', x: 900, y: 600, zoom: 1.3 }]);
660	  u.headline('Tell it what to *write about*', s.W / 2, 178, { size: 84, at: 0.15 });
661	  u.panel(410, 270, 1100, 600, { at: 0.3 }, (x, y, w, h) => {
662	    u.input(x + 56, y + 296, 560, 62, { label: 'Website URL', value: u.typed('https://example.com', '#site', 24), focus: 1, caret: true });
663	    u.button('Get ideas', x + 760, y + 516, 280, 60, { press: u.press('#go') });
664	  });
665	  u.cursor([
666	    { at: 0.5, x: 1500, y: 900 },
667	    { at: s.time('#site') - 0.7, x: 740, y: 596 },
668	    { at: '#site', click: true },
669	  ]);
670	}
671	```
672	
673	Draw the headline after `s.camera()`, or the zoomed screens slide under it.
674	When the camera zooms in, either keep the headline fully in view (zoom up to
675	about 1.1) or zoom far enough that it leaves the frame (about 1.3 and more).
676	In between it gets cut in half.
677	
678	Time every click to a `[#mark]` in the script, so it happens when the voice
679	says it. A cursor move starts at its `at` and arrives `dur` seconds later
680	(0.7 by default), so start the move a little more than `dur` before the
681	click. The first 0.7 seconds of a scene are covered by the transition from
682	the scene before, so put no clicks there.
683	
684	The watermark sits in the bottom right corner. When the camera zooms in, it
685	can cover what is there, so keep important text out of that corner.
686	
687	Use the product's name as plain text in the first frame. The first frame is
688	also the video's cover.
689	
690	**`s.ui` reference.** Positions are in the frame (1920 wide for 16:9). Times
691	take seconds, spoken words or `"#marks"`, like everywhere else.
692	
693	| Call | What it draws |
694	|---|---|
695	| `u.backdrop(color?)`, `u.floaters({ alpha, tint })` | the brand background, and faint cards drifting around the edges (`tint: 'white'` on a colored background) |
696	| `u.panel(x, y, w, h, { at, out, from, rise, sfx, fill, border, lift }, fn)` | a card that pops in at `at`; `fn(x, y, w, h)` draws inside it |
697	| `u.card(x, y, w, h, o)` | a card without an entrance |
698	| `u.browser(x, y, w, h, { url, at }, fn)` | a panel with a browser bar; `fn` gets the page area |
699	| `u.modal(x, y, w, h, { at, out }, fn)`, `u.over(fn)` | a dialog over a dimmed frame, and a layer for your own menus or popups; `check` does not report text in one layer as overlapping text in another |
700	| `u.toast(text, { at, out, icon, tone })` | a message that slides up, with a chime |
701	| `u.headline(text, x, y, { size, at, out, align, weight, italic, color, accent, tracking })` | big text that blurs in word by word; `*stars*` mark words in the `accent` color |
702	| `u.text(text, x, y, { size, weight, color, align, base, font, italic, tracking, alpha, maxW, check })`, `u.para(text, x, y, maxW, o)`, `u.eyebrow(text, x, y)` | text, wrapped text (`\n` starts a new line), and a small uppercase label; `font` is `'ui'`, `'headline'` or a family; `check: false` hides text from `check`; `u.measure(text, size, weight)` gives the width |
703	| `u.button(label, x, y, w, h, { variant, press, icon, loading, color, caps })` | `primary`, `secondary` or `ghost` |
704	| `u.input(x, y, w, h, { label, value, placeholder, focus, caret, icon })`, `u.textarea(x, y, w, h, o)` | a text field, and a taller one where the text wraps |
705	| `u.select(x, y, w, h, { label, value, open, options, hover, selected })` | a dropdown; returns the y of each option row |
706	| `u.toggle(x, y, on)`, `u.checkbox(x, y, on)`, `u.radio(x, y, on)` | `on` goes from 0 to 1, so it can animate |
707	| `u.chip(label, x, y, { selected })`, `u.chipWidth(label, o, selected)` | option chips; a selected chip grows by its check mark, so leave room |
708	| `u.pill(label, x, y, tone, { align, dot, pulse })` | a status badge: `gray`, `blue`, `yellow`, `green`, `red`, `purple` or `accent` |
709	| `u.spinner(x, y, r)`, `u.progress(x, y, w, p)`, `u.avatar(x, y, r, initials, { image })`, `u.icon(name, x, y, size, color)`, `u.lines(x, y, w, n)`, `u.logo(x, y, w)` | small parts; `lines` draws grey bars in place of body text |
710	| `u.pop(at, cx, cy, fn, { sfx })` | pops anything in around a point |
711	| `u.wipe(at, { color })` | the accent color spreads out until it fills the frame |
712	| `u.cursor(keys, { out })` | the mouse; a key `{ at, x, y, dur, arc }` moves it (`arc` bends the path, 0 is straight), `{ at, click: true }` clicks with a sound |
713	| `u.press(at)`, `u.typed(text, at, cps, { gain, sfx })`, `u.stream(text, at, wps)`, `u.count(to, at, dur)` | a button press (0 to 1 and back), text typed so far with typing sounds, text appearing word by word like an AI reply, a number counting up |
714	| `u.colors` | `accent`, `accentDark`, `accentTint`, `background`, `ink`, `text`, `soft`, `muted`, `faint`, `line`, `field`, `surface`, `panel`; `brand.colors` in `video.json` replaces the grays, for example `{ "text": "#141413", "line": "#e8e6dc" }` for a warm brand |
715	
716	`check` sees the text of `s.ui` too. It reports text that runs off the frame
717	(not while the camera zooms in), text that is too small and text that
718	overlaps. A dropdown or dialog may cover what is under it. Look at stills at
719	the moments the camera is zoomed in, and check the cursor does not hide the
720	word it points at.
721	
722	Panels and headlines from `s.ui` make no sound unless you pass `sfx`.
723	`check` does not see clipping: text you hide by clipping still counts, so
724	do not draw text that should not show. Try a product name with its plain
725	spelling first; only respell it when the speech check mishears it.
726	
727	Many sounds under the voice make it hard to follow, and `verify` then
728	understands less of it. Keep long typing quiet (`u.typed(text, at, cps, {
729	gain: 0.25 })`) and give each moment one sound, not three.
730	
731	A blur filter on every frame makes rendering slow. The kit blurs things once
732	and reuses them. Do the same if you add your own blur.
733	
734	## Sizes for each platform
735	
736	Set `size` to the place the video goes. `explainroo formats` lists the sizes.
737	
738	| Size | Pixels | For |
739	|---|---|---|
740	| `youtube` (or `16:9`) | 1920 x 1080 | YouTube and other wide players |
741	| `shorts` | 1080 x 1920 | YouTube Shorts |
742	| `tiktok` | 1080 x 1920 | TikTok |
743	| `reels` | 1080 x 1920 | Instagram and Facebook Reels |
744	| `vertical` | 1080 x 1920 | one file for Shorts, TikTok and Reels |
745	| `instagram` | 1080 x 1350 | Instagram and Facebook feed posts |
746	| `linkedin` | 1080 x 1350 | the LinkedIn feed |
747	| `square` (or `1:1`) | 1080 x 1080 | square posts on X, LinkedIn and Facebook |
748	
749	`9:16`, `4:5` and `WIDTHxHEIGHT` also work.
750	
751	YouTube and LinkedIn take the `.srt` file as captions when you upload the
752	video.
753	
754	Shorts, TikTok and Reels put their own buttons, the account name and the post
755	text on top of the video. With `shorts`, `tiktok`, `reels` and `vertical`,
756	explainroo keeps your content away from those spots. `s.safe` is the part the
757	app leaves free, and `s.cx` and `s.cy` are its center. The captions sit right
758	above the app's bottom band, and the watermark moves to the top right corner.
759	`check` warns about any text outside that area. Plain `9:16` has no such
760	margins, so use it only when the video is not meant for those apps.
761	
762	Vertical videos have little room. Stack things instead of putting them side
763	by side, use fewer words on screen, and place everything from `s.safe`.
764	`explainroo formats` prints the content area of each size in pixels, for
765	example 745 x 746 on TikTok.
766	
767	### Where the captions sit
768	
769	Captions are on by default for vertical, 4:5 and square videos. On Shorts,
770	TikTok and Reels they sit right above the app's bottom band. On 4:5, square
771	and wide videos they sit at the bottom, above the watermark. On a plain
772	`9:16` video they sit about a quarter of the way up. In every case `s.safe`
773	ends above them. So anything placed inside `s.safe` stays clear of them, and
774	`check` warns about text in the caption band. Do not put the same sentence on
775	screen as text while the captions show it.
776	
777	The first frame is often the cover picture on social apps. When the first
778	elements draw themselves in, frame one is empty. Give the title
779	`enter: 'none'` so it is there from the first frame, or pick another cover
780	frame in the app.
781	
782	## One project, several sizes
783	
784	Use `--size <size>` on `preview`, `still`, `sheet`, `check` and `render`
785	to use another size for one run. It leaves `video.json` alone. The narration
786	and word times are shared between sizes. Captions set to `"auto"` follow
787	the size you choose.
788	
789	Place things from `s.safe`, `s.cx`, `s.cy`, `s.row`, `s.col` and `s.grid`
790	instead of fixed pixel positions. Use `s.portrait` to stack things in tall
791	frames. Keep text sizes relative to the frame, for example `s.W * 0.05`.
792	`examples/merge-vs-rebase` works in `9:16` and in `youtube`; its scenes.js
793	shows one way to do it.
794	
795	```js
796	intro(s) {
797	  const cells = s.grid(s.portrait ? 1 : 2, s.portrait ? 2 : 1);
798	  ['Question', 'Answer'].forEach((label, i) => {
799	    const { x, y, w, h } = cells[i];
800	    s.box(label, { x, y, w: w * 0.8, h: h * 0.6,
801	      size: s.W * 0.05, at: i ? '#answer' : 0 });
802	  });
803	}
804	```
805	
806	For every size, run `check --size <size>` and look at
807	`sheet --size <size>`. Fix cut-off text and overlaps. Then run
808	`render --size <size>` for each size.
809	
810	`render --size tiktok` writes `out/video-tiktok.mp4` and matching subtitle,
811	cover and chapter files. With `--draft`, it writes `draft-tiktok.mp4`.
812	Stills go in `out/stills-tiktok/`. A sheet is `out/sheet-tiktok.jpg`, or
813	`out/sheet-intro-tiktok.jpg` with `--scene intro`. Ratios use a hyphen in
814	file names, for example `video-9-16.mp4`. Without `--size`, names stay the
815	same. `render --out` still sets the file name you choose.
816	
817	## Pace
818	
819	`pace` makes the whole video quicker or slower. At 1.3 the voice speaks about
820	30% faster. The voice model does not speed up exactly, so check the length
821	`voice` reports. The pauses, the time before and after each scene, the transitions and
822	every animation also get 30% shorter. Word and marker cues follow the voice.
823	So if you tie pictures to words, the rhythm stays right at any pace. Times in
824	seconds are real seconds of the scene, like `s.t` and `s.cue()`. So
825	`at: s.cue('word') + 0.3` means 0.3 seconds after the word at any pace. A
826	fixed time like `at: 4` does not move with the pace. That is one more reason
827	to use cues. 1 is the normal pace. 1.15 to 1.3 suits social media and viewers
828	who know the topic. Above 1.4 the video gets hard to follow. `speed` changes
829	only the voice. Its default is 0.9 for Kokoro and 1 for the other sources.
830	
831	## The watermark
832	
833	Every video gets a small "explainroo.com" in the bottom right corner. On
834	Shorts, TikTok and Reels it sits in the top right corner, because the app
835	covers the bottom one. The user can turn it off with `"watermark": false` in
836	`video.json`, or change it to their own text. If the user asks about it, tell
837	them it is their choice, and that keeping it helps more people find this free
838	project.
839	
840	## Project files and settings
841	
842	```
843	videos/<name>/
844	  video.json    look, size, language, voice, music, captions, watermark
845	  script.md     the narration, one "## scene-id" per scene
846	  scenes.js     one drawing function per scene
847	  assets/       images: screenshots, logos, generated illustrations
848	  build/        voice, timing and audio made by explainroo (safe to delete)
849	  out/          video.mp4, draft.mp4, stills/, sheets, report.json
850	```
851	
852	`video.json` settings, all optional:
853	
854	| Setting | Default | What it does |
855	|---|---|---|
856	| `title` | from the script | shown in the preview |
857	| `theme` | `paper` | a built-in look, or a relative path to a `.json` look file (see Custom looks) |
858	| `size` | `16:9` | a platform (`youtube`, `shorts`, `tiktok`, `reels`, `vertical`, `instagram`, `linkedin`, `square`), `16:9`, `9:16`, `1:1`, `4:5` or `WIDTHxHEIGHT` |
859	| `fps` | 30 | 24, 25, 30, 50 or 60 |
860	| `language` | `en` | narration language: `en`, `es`, `fr`, `it`, `pt-br`, `hi`, `ja` or `zh`; see Videos in other languages |
861	| `voice` | the language's voice | a Kokoro id or a provider object for Kokoro, ElevenLabs or a recording; English uses `af_heart`; see Voices |
862	| `speed` | 0.9 for Kokoro, 1 otherwise | voice speed only, 0.6 to 1.6; ElevenLabs limits speed times pace to 0.7 through 1.2 |
863	| `pace` | 1 | speed of the whole video (voice, pauses, animations), 0.7 to 1.6 |
864	| `music` | `true` | `true` (the look's style), `warm`, `upbeat`, `calm`, `tech`, `playful`, `{ "style", "volume" }` or `false` |
865	| `sfx` | `true` | sound effects: `true`, `"minimal"` or `false` |
866	| `captions` | `"auto"` | text of the narration at the bottom; auto turns it on for vertical and square videos |
867	| `cover` | `"auto"` | full-size JPEG, without captions, at the end of the first scene, or a still spec: `12.5`, `"intro"`, `"intro@2.4"`, `"intro@end"` |
868	| `transition` | `"auto"` | the look's default, or `fade`, `slide`, `wipe`, `zoom`, `brush`, `cut` |
869	| `camera` | `"auto"` | `"auto"` films each scene with a camera that follows the narration; `"still"` keeps every frame as written |
870	| `motionBlur` | `true` | motion blur on moving frames in the final render |
871	| `lead`, `hold`, `end` | 0.35, 0.7, 1.4 | seconds before the voice, after it, and extra at the very end |
872	| `sentenceGap`, `paragraphGap` | 0.3, 0.55 | pauses in generated narration; recordings keep their own pauses |
873	| `loudness` | -14 | target loudness in LUFS |
874	| `boil` | 0 | redraws per second of hand-drawn lines; 0 keeps them still |
875	| `watermark` | `"explainroo.com"` | small text in a corner, or `false` |
876	| `images` | none | `{ "model": "best" or "cheap", "style": "..." }` |
877	| `brand` | none | colors, fonts and logo for `s.ui`, see [Product demos](#product-demos) |
878	| `fonts` | none | the video's own font files, see [Product demos](#product-demos) |
879	
880	The looks:
881	
882	| Look | What it looks like | Transition | Music |
883	|---|---|---|---|
884	| `paper` | marker drawings on warm paper | brush | warm |
885	| `clean` | flat cards with soft shadows | slide | upbeat |
886	| `chalk` | chalk on a green board | brush | calm |
887	| `blueprint` | white drawings on blueprint blue | wipe | tech |
888	| `midnight` | dark background with glowing colors | zoom | tech |
889	| `editorial` | serif headlines and fine lines on warm paper, like a magazine | fade | calm |
890	
891	### Custom looks
892	
893	A look file gives a project its own colors, fonts and texture without
894	changing explainroo. Set `"theme": "../brand/acme-look.json"` in `video.json`.
895	The path is relative to the video folder, so a file outside it can give
896	several videos the same look.
897	
898	```json
899	{
900	  "extends": "clean",
901	  "name": "acme",
902	  "bg": "#fbfaf7",
903	  "ink": "#1d1b16",
904	  "accent": "orange",
905	  "colors": { "orange": "#e8590c" },
906	  "background": "dots",
907	  "transition": "fade",
908	  "music": "calm"
909	}
910	```
911	
912	`extends` names the built-in look to start from. Every other key is
913	optional and changes only what it lists: `name`, `dark`, `bg`, `ink`, `muted`,
914	`surface`, `accent`, `colors`, `fonts`, `background`, `stroke`, `radius`,
915	`transition`, `music`, `code`, `terminal` and `caption`. In the nested ones,
916	like `colors` or `caption`, list only the values you change. `name` defaults
917	to the file name and cannot be a built-in name. Set `"dark": true` when you
918	give a light look a dark `bg`, so fills and shadows suit it.
919	
920	Use colors like `"#e8590c"`. `caption.bg` and `code.highlight` also accept
921	`rgba(...)`. `accent` names one of the ten colors: red, orange, yellow,
922	green, teal, blue, purple, pink, gray or ink.
923	
924	`fonts` sets the `display`, `body`, `hand` and `mono` roles. Each takes a
925	`family` and a numeric `weight`, for example
926	`"body": { "family": "Inter", "weight": 500 }`. Families must be built in
927	or listed in `video.json` under `fonts`. The look file does not load fonts.
928	
929	`background` picks a texture: `paper` (paper grain), `dots` (a dot grid),
930	`chalk` (board smudges), `grid` (drafting lines), `glow` (two glows) or
931	`plain` (one flat color). Each uses your `bg`. Leave it out to keep the base
932	texture. `transition` and `music` set the look's defaults. Settings in
933	`video.json` still win. `preview` picks up look edits, and `check` warns
934	when `ink` or `muted` has too little contrast against `bg`.
935	
936	## Commands
937	
938	| Command | What it does |
939	|---|---|
940	| `init <dir>` | creates a project (`--theme`, `--size`, `--pace`, `--language`, `--voice`, `--title`) |
941	| `voice [project]` | makes the narration and the word times, saved per scene so only changed scenes are made again |
942	| `preview [project]` | a live preview in the browser that reloads when you save (`--size`) |
943	| `still [project] [times]` | PNG pictures at `12.5`, `scene`, `scene@2.4` or `scene@end` (`--size`, `--transparent` keeps alpha) |
944	| `sheet [project]` | one image with many frames of the video or of one scene (`--scene`, `--every`, `--size`) |
945	| `check [project]` | finds layout, timing and pronunciation problems (`--size`) |
946	| `render [project]` | the video plus subtitle files, chapters and a cover (`--format mp4`, `gif`, `webm` or `mov`, `--transparent`, `--size`, `--draft`, `--from`, `--to`, `--workers`, `--out`) |
947	| `verify [project]` | checks the finished MP4, WebM or MOV: loudness, black frames, silence, clear voice (`--file` picks another file) |
948	| `export [project]` | writes a web player folder (`--out dir`, `--json`), by default `out/player/` |
949	| `capture [project] <url>` | saves screenshots, wording, colors, fonts and logos from a live site (`--mobile`, `--json`) |
950	| `image [project] <name> "<prompt>"` | makes an illustration with OpenRouter |
951	| `images [project]` | lists the images and what they cost |
952	| `voices`, `say "text"` | lists Kokoro voices with their languages (`--language es`, `ja` or `zh` filters voices), or makes a sample in the chosen voice's language |
953	| `voices [project] --elevenlabs` | lists the names, ids and labels available to the key (`--json` also works) |
954	| `themes`, `formats`, `icons <word>` | lists the looks and the sizes, searches the 1,854 icons |
955	| `doctor` | checks Node, ffmpeg, Chrome, the models, and espeak-ng and Python with misaki for other languages (`--fetch` downloads only English models) |
956	
957	The extra files share the video's name, also with `--out`: `video.srt`,
958	`video.vtt`, `video.cover.jpg` and `video.chapters.txt`. A GIF has no sound,
959	runs at 15 frames per second and is at most 960 pixels on its long side.
960	With `--out`, the file ending must match the format. A render of part of the
961	video (`--from`, `--to`) skips the chapters and the cover. A video without
962	narration gets no subtitle files.
963	
964	### Transparent video for editors
965	
966	To put a diagram or a lower third over camera footage, render with an alpha
967	channel: `render --transparent --format mov` for video editors (ProRes 4444)
968	or `--format webm` for the web (VP9). The look's background texture is left
969	out. Everything the scene paints stays, including `s.bg()` and
970	`s.ui.backdrop()`, and cards, captions and the watermark keep their own
971	backgrounds. Keep elements readable on footage: use cards or the `bg` option
972	of text.
973	
974	The files are `out/video.mov` or `out/video.webm` (`draft.*` with
975	`--draft`), and the cover stays an opaque JPEG. `slide`, `wipe` and `brush`
976	transitions become `fade` in a transparent video. Check frames with
977	`still --transparent`, which keeps the alpha, and the file with
978	`verify --file out/video.mov`, which skips the black-frame check for
979	transparent video. Without `--transparent`, WebM and MOV are ordinary opaque
980	videos.
981	
982	## Web player
983	
984	Use `export` for a video on a docs site or in an iframe. The canvas draws
985	at the screen's resolution, so text stays sharp when the player grows.
986	The folder has its own engine, fonts, assets, soundtrack, cover and subtitles.
987	It needs no explainroo server. The voice is made first if needed.
988	
989	```bash
990	node bin/explainroo.js export videos/my-video
991	```
992	
993	Serve `out/player/` with any static web server. Opening `index.html` as a
994	local file does not work. Copy the folder to your site, keeping its files
995	together. It works in a subfolder too.
996	
997	For a 1920 x 1080 video, embed it like this. Change the aspect ratio to
998	match your video's width and height.
999	
1000	```html
1001	<div style="aspect-ratio: 1920 / 1080; width: 100%">
1002	  <iframe src="/videos/my-video/index.html" title="My video"
1003	    style="width: 100%; height: 100%; border: 0"
1004	    allow="autoplay; fullscreen" allowfullscreen></iframe>
1005	</div>
1006	```
1007	
1008	`index.html?autoplay=1&muted=1` starts muted playback, subject to the
1009	browser's autoplay rules. `index.html?t=12.5` starts at 12.5 seconds.
1010	Click the picture to play or pause. Space or K does the same. The arrow
1011	keys seek five seconds. F opens fullscreen, and M mutes the sound.
1012	CC shows text captions above the controls. It starts off and remembers
1013	your choice in the browser. Captions drawn in the canvas stay as they are.
1014	
1015	Clips use the browser's video decoder. Use a format the browser can play.
1016	Their timing can differ a little from the MP4. Math formulas are included
1017	as typeset paths. Project modules must use relative local imports. Dotfiles,
1018	JSON and text files from the project are kept out of the export.
1019	
1020	The MP4 remains the file to upload to video platforms.
1021	
1022	## Scene API
1023	
1024	Each scene function draws one frame. explainroo calls it for every frame with
1025	a fresh `s`, so everything in the scene depends only on the time. Do not keep
1026	values between calls and do not use timers. Give each element an `at` time,
1027	and the look animates it in. Give it an `out` time, and the look animates it
1028	out.
1029	
1030	```js
1031	export default {
1032	  intro(s) {
1033	    s.title('How DNS works', { at: 0 });
1034	    s.icon('globe', { y: 700, at: 'browser' });
1035	  },
1036	};
1037	```
1038	
1039	The canvas is 1920 x 1080 for 16:9. It is 1080 x 1920 for the vertical sizes,
1040	1080 x 1080 for square, and 1080 x 1350 for `instagram`, `linkedin` and 4:5.
1041	Positions are in pixels, counted from the top left corner. `x` and `y` are
1042	the center of an element unless the method says otherwise.
1043	
1044	### Time
1045	
1046	| Member | Meaning |
1047	|---|---|
1048	| `s.t` | seconds since the scene started |
1049	| `s.T` | seconds since the video started |
1050	| `s.dur` | length of the scene in seconds |
1051	| `s.voice` | `{ start, end }` of the narration in the scene |
1052	| `s.words` | the spoken words: `{ text, start, end }` in scene seconds |
