1053	| `s.cue(word, n = 1)` | when the n-th time a word or phrase is spoken starts |
1054	| `s.cueEnd(word, n = 1)` | when it ends |
1055	| `s.mark(name)` | the time of a `[#name]` marker |
1056	| `s.time(v)` | turns a number, a word or `"#marker"` into seconds |
1057	| `s.pace` | the video's pace (1 is normal) |
1058	| `s.p(at, dur = 0.6, ease = 'inOut')` | 0 to 1 progress for your own animation |
1059	| `s.since(at)`, `s.between(a, b)` | seconds since a time, or whether now is between two times |
1060	| `s.video` | `{ duration, frames, fps, width, height, scenes }` of the whole video |
1061	
1062	Every `at` and `out` accepts seconds (`2.4`), a spoken word (`'resolver'`) or
1063	a marker (`'#ask'`). Word cues ignore case and punctuation. A phrase works
1064	too. A wrong word or marker stops with an error that lists the closest words.
1065	
1066	An easing sets how a movement speeds up and slows down. The easing names are
1067	`linear`, `in`, `out`, `inOut`, `outBack`, `outElastic`, `outQuart` and
1068	`inOutSine`. `s.ease.out(p)`, `s.lerp(a, b, p)` and `s.clamp(v, lo, hi)` are
1069	there for your own math.
1070	
1071	### Layout
1072	
1073	| Member | Meaning |
1074	|---|---|
1075	| `s.W`, `s.H`, `s.cx`, `s.cy` | canvas size and its center (on Shorts, TikTok and Reels the center of `s.safe`) |
1076	| `s.portrait` | true when the frame is taller than wide |
1077	| `s.safe` | `{ x, y, w, h, left, top, right, bottom }`: the area for content (a 7% margin, or the part a platform leaves free) |
1078	| `s.row(n, { width, x })` | n x positions spread over a width |
1079	| `s.col(n, { height, y })` | n y positions spread over a height, centered in `s.safe` |
1080	| `s.grid(cols, rows, { x, y, w, h, gap })` | cells `{ x, y, w, h }`, row by row, filling `s.safe` |
1081	| `s.get(id)` | size and position of an element drawn earlier with that `id` |
1082	
1083	Most elements return `{ x, y, w, h, left, right, top, bottom }`, so you can
1084	place the next thing below or beside them.
1085	
1086	### Options most elements take
1087	
1088	| Option | Meaning |
1089	|---|---|
1090	| `at`, `out` | when it appears and when it leaves |
1091	| `enter` | `draw`, `write`, `type`, `words`, `sync`, `pop`, `rise`, `fade`, `zoom`, `drop`, `slide-left`, `slide-right`, `slide-up`, `slide-down`, `none` |
1092	| `exit` | `fade`, `pop`, `rise`, `drop`, `slide-left`, `slide-right`, `none` |
1093	| `dur` | length of the entrance in seconds (shortened by the pace) |
1094	| `outDur` | length of the exit in seconds (0.45) |
1095	
1096	`out` is when the exit starts, so an element is still fading while the next
1097	one comes in. To swap one label for another in the same place, give the old
1098	one `exit: 'none'` and the new one the same time as its `at`.
1099	| `id` | a name for arrows, `annotate` and `s.get` |
1100	| `color` | a color name (`blue`, `accent`, `ink`, `muted`, ...) or any CSS color |
1101	| `opacity`, `scale`, `rotate` | extra changes, `rotate` in degrees |
1102	| `float` | pixels of slow drift that keeps a still element alive |
1103	| `sfx` | the sound on entrance, or `false` |
1104	
1105	In `paper`, `chalk` and `blueprint`, shapes are drawn line by line and text
1106	is written out. In `clean` and `midnight`, shapes pop and text rises. In
1107	`editorial`, shapes fade in and text rises. Use
1108	`at: -1` for things that were already on screen in the previous scene. They
1109	are there from the start and make no sound.
1110	
1111	### Text
1112	
1113	```js
1114	s.title('How DNS works', { at: 0 });                     // display font, 104 px, at 44% height
1115	s.subtitle('the phone book of the internet', { at: 1 }); // 46 px, muted, at 60% height
1116	s.text('Every site has an *address*', { y: 300, size: 64, at: 'address' });
1117	s.note('about 20 ms', { x: 1400, y: 820, at: 3 });        // 32 px, muted
1118	```
1119	
1120	`s.text(str, options)` takes these options: `x`, `y`, `size` (48), `font`
1121	(`display`, `body`, `hand`, `mono`), `weight` or `bold: true`, `align`
1122	(`left` means x is the left edge), `valign` (`top` means y is the top),
1123	`maxWidth`, `lineHeight`, `color`, `mark` (color of `*starred*` words), `bg`
1124	(a card behind the text), `padding`, `radius`, `border`. Text can also enter
1125	with `write`, `type` (with `cps`), `words` and `sync`. `sync` shows each word
1126	as the voice says it. Words in `*stars*` get the accent color. `\n` starts a
1127	new line.
1128	
1129	### Math
1130	
1131	```js
1132	s.math('E = mc^2', { y: 420, size: 96, at: 'energy' });
1133	s.math(String.raw`\int_0^1 x^2\,dx = \tfrac{1}{3}`, { x: 960, y: 700, size: 72, color: 'accent', at: '#integral', id: 'area' });
1134	```
1135	
1136	`s.math(tex, options)` draws a TeX formula. Options: `x`, `y` (the center),
1137	`size` (72), `color` (ink), `align` (`center` or `left`, where x is the left
1138	edge), `display` (true for display style), and the common element options.
1139	It returns a box like text. Its `id` works with arrows and `s.annotate`.
1140	In `paper`, `chalk` and `blueprint`, the outlines draw first, then fill in.
1141	Other looks use their text entrance. You can also use `fade`, `pop` or `rise`.
1142	
1143	Double each backslash in a regular JS string, for example `'\\frac{1}{2}'`.
1144	`String.raw` keeps backslashes as written, as in the second example above.
1145	Only fixed TeX strings work. Always call `s.math`, and use `at` and `out` for
1146	timing. `check` reports formulas that change with `s.t` or appear only later.
1147	Formulas are typeset on this computer and cached in `build/math/`.
1148	
1149	### Shapes and arrows
1150	
1151	```js
1152	s.box('Resolver', { id: 'res', x: 960, y: 540, icon: 'server', color: 'blue', at: 'resolver' });
1153	s.circle({ id: 'you', x: 400, y: 540, r: 90, label: 'You', at: 0.5 });
1154	s.arrow('you', 'res', { label: 'asks', at: 'asks' });
1155	s.arrow([300, 900], [1600, 900], { bend: 0.3, dashed: true, at: 5 });
1156	s.line([[200, 800], [900, 700]], { color: 'accent', at: 2 });
1157	s.path('M0 0 C 200 -150 400 150 600 0', { x: 660, y: 540, at: 3 });
1158	```
1159	
1160	`s.box(label, options)` is a card that fits its label. Options: `w`, `h`,
1161	`minW`, `minH`, `size`, `font`, `icon`, `iconSize`, `iconColor`, `color`
1162	(outline and a light fill), `fill`, `stroke`, `width`, `dashed`,
1163	`border: false`, `radius`, `shape: 'ellipse'`, `textColor`, `fillStyle`
1164	(hand-drawn looks: `solid`, `hachure`, `cross-hatch`, `zigzag`, `dots`).
1165	`s.circle({ r, label })` takes the same options.
1166	
1167	`s.line(points, options)` and `s.path(d, options)` take `color`, `width`,
1168	`dashed`, `fill` and `glow`. `s.path` also takes `fillStyle`, and
1169	`single: true` draws a hand-drawn path with one stroke instead of two.
1170	
1171	`s.arrow(from, to, options)` draws an arrow. `from` and `to` are `[x, y]`,
1172	`{ x, y }` or the `id` of an element drawn earlier. With an `id`, the arrow
1173	stops at the edge of that element. Options: `bend` (about -1 to 1), `head`
1174	(`end`, `start`, `both`, `none`), `label`, `labelSize`, `labelColor`,
1175	`labelFont` (the handwritten font by default; `'body'` suits `clean`),
1176	`labelOffset`, `gap`, `dashed`, `width`, `headSize`. `s.connect` does the
1177	same.
1178	
1179	### Icons, images and video clips
1180	
1181	```js
1182	s.icon('database', { x: 1500, y: 540, size: 140, color: 'purple', at: 'database' });
1183	s.icon('lock', { bg: 'circle', color: 'green', label: 'Encrypted', at: 4 });
1184	s.image('assets/app.png', { w: 1100, frame: 'browser', url: 'app.example.com', at: 0.5 });
1185	s.image('assets/cables.png', { w: 1000, frame: 'card', at: 'cables', kenburns: true });
1186	```
1187	
1188	The 1,854 icons come from Lucide. Search them with `explainroo icons <word>`.
1189	Old Lucide names work too. Icon options: `size` (120), `color`, `weight` (2),
1190	`bg` (`true`, `'circle'`, `'square'` or a color), `bgScale`, `label`,
1191	`labelSize`, `labelColor`, `labelFont`.
1192	
1193	`s.image(src, options)` draws a file from `assets/`. Options: `w` and/or `h`
1194	(the image keeps its shape), `fit` (`cover` or `contain`), `radius`, `frame`
1195	(`none`, `card`, `browser`, `window`, `phone`), `url` (for the browser frame),
1196	`border`, `shadow: false`, `kenburns: true` for a slow zoom.
1197	
1198	`s.clip(src, options)` draws a video from `assets/`. It takes the same
1199	layout, frame and timing options as `s.image` and returns the same box.
1200	Use `.mp4`, `.mov`, `.webm` or `.m4v` files.
1201	
1202	```js
1203	s.clip('assets/signup.mp4', { w: 1200, frame: 'browser', url: 'app.example.com', at: 'signup' });
1204	s.clip('assets/demo.mov', { x: 1400, y: 540, h: 600, from: 12.5, speed: 1.5, at: '#demo', out: 9 });
1205	```
1206	
1207	- `from` (0) is the second in the clip where playback starts.
1208	- `speed` (1) goes from 0.25 to 4. Playback starts at `at`.
1209	- `loop` (false) repeats the whole clip. Otherwise it holds the last frame.
1210	  `check` gives a hint when that freeze lasts more than a second.
1211	- `volume` (0) goes from 0 to 1. Sound plays only at `speed: 1`.
1212	  It stops at `out`, the clip's end or the scene's end. Loops repeat the
1213	  sound too. Music ducks under clip sound as it does under the voice.
1214	
1215	Frames are extracted once at the project's fps and saved in `build/clips/`.
1216	They are at most 1920 pixels wide. Renders, stills and sheets use these
1217	frames. The preview may show a nearby frame while the right one loads.
1218	`s.video` still holds the whole video's duration, frames, fps and size.
1219	Playback starts at `at`, so `at: -1` starts a clip one second into it. To
1220	show a clip from its first frame when the scene starts, use
1221	`at: 0, enter: 'none'`. `check` cannot read text inside a clip, so look at
1222	stills of those moments.
1223	
1224	### Pointing things out
1225	
1226	```js
1227	s.text('It is *not* magic', { id: 'claim', y: 400, at: 0 });
1228	s.annotate('claim', { type: 'underline', at: 'magic' });
1229	s.annotate({ x: 960, y: 700, w: 400, h: 90 }, { type: 'circle', color: 'red', at: 3 });
1230	```
1231	
1232	`s.annotate(target, options)` marks an element or a box. `type` is
1233	`underline`, `circle`, `box`, `highlight`, `strike`, `cross` or `bracket`.
1234	Options: `color`, `padding`, `width`, `dur`.
1235	
1236	### Lists, numbers and charts
1237	
1238	```js
1239	s.list(['Browser cache', 'Resolver', 'Root server'], { x: 360, y: 280, at: 'first', stagger: 1.1 });
1240	s.number(1500000, { suffix: ' requests', at: 'million', y: 480 });
1241	s.bars([{ label: '2023', value: 19 }, { label: '2024', value: 31 }], { at: 1, suffix: 'k' });
1242	s.lineChart([3, 5, 4, 8, 13, 21], { labels: ['M', 'T', 'W', 'T', 'F', 'S'], at: 1 });
1243	s.pie([{ label: 'Images', value: 55 }, { label: 'Other', value: 45 }], { donut: 0.55, at: 1 });
1244	```
1245	
1246	- `s.list(items, options)`: items are text or `{ text, at, icon, color, out }`.
1247	  `x` is the left edge and `y` the top. Options: `size` (50), `width`, `gap`,
1248	  `bullet` (`dot`, `dash`, `number`, `check`, `arrow` or an icon name),
1249	  `bulletColor`, `at` (a start time or a list of times), `stagger` (0.7),
1250	  `enter`.
1251	- `s.number(value, options)` counts up with ticking sounds. Options: `from`,
1252	  `dur` (1.4), `prefix`, `suffix`, `decimals`, `separator`, `group: false`,
1253	  plus the text options.
1254	- `s.bars(data, options)`: items are `{ label, value, color, at }`. A bar
1255	  with its own `at` grows when that word is spoken. Options: `x`, `y`, `w`,
1256	  `h`, `max`, `stagger`, `growDur`, `values: false`, `prefix`, `suffix`,
1257	  `format(v)`, `labelSize`, `valueSize`, `fillStyle`.
1258	- `s.lineChart(values, options)`: `x`, `y`, `w`, `h`, `min`, `max`,
1259	  `labels`, `dots: false`, `area: false`, `color`, `dur`.
1260	- `s.pie(data, options)`: `x`, `y`, `r`, `donut` (0 to 0.8), `labels: false`,
1261	  `labelOffset`, `dur`.
1262	
1263	### Code and terminal
1264	
1265	```js
1266	s.code("const res = await fetch(url);", { lang: 'js', title: 'app.js', at: 0.5 });
1267	s.terminal(['$ npm install', 'added 42 packages in 3s'], { at: 1 });
1268	```
1269	
1270	`s.code(source, options)` shows code. Options: `x` and `y` (the center of
1271	the window), `lang` (`js`, `ts`, `py`,
1272	`go`, `rust`, `php`, `sh`, `sql`, `json`, `md`), `size` (32), `w`, `title`
1273	(or `false`), `lineNumbers: false`, `reveal` (`type`, `lines`, `none`),
1274	`cps`, `lineDelay`, `highlight` (line numbers) and `highlightAt`. `check`
1275	reports lines that are wider than the window.
1276	
1277	Use steps to show a change, a refactor or a fix:
1278	
1279	```js
1280	s.code([
1281	  { code: 'const total = items.length;', at: 0.5 },
1282	  { code: 'const total = items\n  .filter((i) => i.active)\n  .length;', at: 'filter', highlight: [2] },
1283	  { code: 'const activeCount = items\n  .filter((i) => i.active)\n  .length;', at: '#rename' },
1284	], { lang: 'js', title: 'stats.js' });
1285	```
1286	
1287	Parts that stay slide to their new place, removed parts fade out and new
1288	parts fade in. Each later step starts at its own `at`, and the change takes
1289	`morphDur` seconds (0.8, shortened by `pace`). The element's `at` defaults to
1290	the first step's. `reveal` applies to the first step only. A step's
1291	`highlight` replaces the earlier one. The window is as wide as the widest
1292	step, up to 86% of the frame, unless you set `w`, and its height follows the
1293	line count. `check` checks the lines of every step.
1294	
1295	`s.terminal(lines, options)` shows a terminal. Lines that start with `$ ` are
1296	typed as commands. Other lines are output. A line can also be `{ cmd, at }`
1297	or `{ out, at, color }`. Options: `w`, `size`, `rows`, `title`, `prompt`,
1298	`cps` (22), `outputDelay`, `lineGap`.
1299	
1300	### Camera, groups and your own drawing
1301	
1302	- `s.camera([{ at, x, y, zoom, dur, rotate }])` moves the view by hand
1303	  instead of the automatic camera (see The camera). Call it first in the
1304	  scene function. `s.camera([])` keeps the scene still.
1305	- `s.focus(target, { at, out })` points the automatic camera at an element
1306	  or a region on a cue, for example one row of a table. `target` is an `id`
1307	  drawn earlier, `{ x, y, w, h }` (center and size) or `[x, y, w, h]`. It
1308	  draws nothing. Highlighted code lines and terminal commands get a
1309	  close-up on their own when they are small in the shot.
1310	- `s.group({ x, y, scale, rotate, at, out, enter }, () => { ... })` moves a
1311	  set of elements together. Inside the group, positions count from `x`, `y`.
1312	- `s.draw({ x, y, at, out }, (ctx, life) => { ... })` gives you the canvas 2D
1313	  context, so you can draw anything the other methods cannot. `life.p` is the
1314	  entrance progress from 0 to 1, and `life.local` the seconds since `at`, for
1315	  motion that keeps going. `s.ctx` is also available.
1316	- `s.burst({ x, y, at, count, colors })` fires confetti.
1317	- `s.bg(color)` paints over the background.
1318	- `s.rand(i)`, `s.noise(x)` and `s.wiggle(amount, speed)` give random values
1319	  that come out the same every time you render.
1320	
1321	### Sound
1322	
1323	Elements make a sound when they enter. The look picks the sound. Drawn lines
1324	scribble, cards pop, arrows whoosh, list items play rising notes, numbers
1325	tick, typing clicks, and scenes change with a swipe. `sfx: false` silences
1326	one element. `"sfx": "minimal"` in `video.json` keeps only the transitions
1327	and the sounds you add yourself.
1328	
1329	`s.sfx(name, at, { gain, dur, pitch })` adds a sound: `pop`, `click`,
1330	`whoosh`, `swipe`, `tick`, `type`, `ding`, `chime`, `thud`, `scribble`,
1331	`chalk`, `rise`, `sparkle`, `blip`, `error`. `dur` sets the length of `type`,
1332	`scribble`, `chalk` and `rise` (`rise` ends at `at + dur`). The music is made
1333	to fit the video, and it gets quieter while the voice speaks.
1334	
1335	### Colors and fonts
1336	
1337	For product demos in a brand's own colors and fonts, see
1338	[Product demos](#product-demos).
1339	
1340	Color names: `accent`, `ink`, `muted`, `bg`, `surface`, `red`, `orange`,
1341	`yellow`, `green`, `teal`, `blue`, `purple`, `pink`, `gray`. Each look has its
1342	own version of each color, so scenes keep working when you change the look.
1343	`s.color(name)` returns the CSS color and `s.tint(name)` the light fill. The
1344	font roles are `display`, `body`, `hand` and `mono`.
1345	
1346	## Things that break
1347	
1348	- Calling an element only sometimes, like `if (s.t > 3) s.box(...)`. Always
1349	  call it, and use `at` and `out` for the timing. The sound effects and the
1350	  checks depend on this.
1351	- Changing the TeX in `s.math` over time. Use fixed strings for each formula,
1352	  and give each one its own `at` and `out`.
1353	- An arrow or annotation that points to an `id` drawn later in the function.
1354	- `s.camera()` after other elements. It has to come first.
1355	- An `at` later than the end of the scene. Make the scene longer with
1356	  `{hold=2}` or `{min=6}` in the script.
1357	- Random values from anything other than `s.rand()`, `s.noise()` or
1358	  `Math.random()` inside the scene function. Only those three come out the
1359	  same on every render.
1360	- Text in AI images. Put words on screen with `s.text`.
1361	- A cue word that is spoken twice. `at: 'writes'` means the first time. Use
1362	  `s.cue('writes', 2)` or a `[#marker]` for a later one. `check` lists such
1363	  words as hints.
1364	- Apostrophes in cue words. Cues ignore punctuation, so "visitor's" is heard
1365	  as "visitors". `at: 'visitor'` then waits for the next plain "visitor".
1366	  Use a `[#marker]` there.
1367	- Elements inside `s.group`. Each one makes its sound at its own `at`, even
1368	  while the group is still hidden. Give them the group's `at`, or
1369	  `sfx: false`.
1370	- Words that sound like other words. The speech check hears "won" as "one"
1371	  and "plain" as "plane", and `{shown|spoken}` cannot fix that. Reword the
1372	  sentence. For acronyms that are spelled out letter by letter, write
1373	  `{API|A, P, I}`. Do not write `A.P.I.`, because the voice reads the dots
1374	  like a web address.
1375	- Growing a shape by changing `w` or `h` over time in the hand-drawn looks.
1376	  The outline is drawn fresh every frame, so it wobbles. Animate `scale`
1377	  instead.
1378	- A scene change in the middle of one diagram. The default transition of
1379	  `clean` slides the whole frame. Give scenes that share a diagram
1380	  `{transition=fade}` or `{transition=cut}`.
1381	- Arrows from an icon that has a label. The `id` of an icon covers the icon
1382	  only, not its label, so an arrow that leaves downward crosses the label.
1383	- Colored text in `paper` and `clean`. Green, orange and red are light enough
1384	  for shapes but often too light for text. `check` warns about low contrast.
1385	  It suggests a readable color and shows its contrast ratio.
1386	- Long video clips fill `build/clips/` with frames. Trim footage to the part
1387	  you show before you add it. Deleting `build/clips/` is safe.
1388	- Screen recordings often have a variable frame rate. explainroo converts
1389	  them to the project's fps, so a frame can repeat or drop. Record at a fixed
1390	  frame rate when the motion matters.
1391	- Exact geometry in `paper`, `chalk` and `blueprint`. Their outlines are
1392	  hand drawn, so a circle's edge wanders a little from its radius. When a
1393	  line has to meet an outline exactly, like a radius that ends on the
1394	  circle, draw both with `s.draw`.
1395	- A render that stops with a closed browser or `EPIPE` on a busy computer.
1396	  Run it again with fewer pages, for example `render --workers 2`.
1397	
1398	`check` looks at text and math formulas. It finds text off the frame, too
1399	close to the edge (3.5%), too small, with low contrast (against the page, or
1400	against the text's own `bg`), on top of other text, or under the captions.
1401	It does not see shapes, arrows and icons, or drawings made with `s.draw`.
1402	Look at the stills for those.
1403	
1404	## Working on explainroo itself
1405	
1406	- `src/` is the part that runs in Node: script, voice, word timing, timeline,
1407	  server, rendering, checks and images. `engine/` is the part that runs in
1408	  the browser page: the scene API (`stage.js`), drawing (`pen.js`), looks,
1409	  transitions, captions and audio (`engine/audio/`).
1410	- Run `npm test` after changes. After engine changes, also render the videos
1411	  in `examples/` and look at their stills and sheets.
1412	- `dev/audio-lab.mjs` renders every music style and sound and measures them.
1413	- `npm run build:icons` rebuilds the icons from `lucide-static`, and
1414	  `npm run build:fonts` downloads the fonts again.
1415

## 0028 User — 2026-11-08T19:07:57.204Z

### Tool result: toolu_0118nmvYsXq39nU231bPmX52

1	# SC2002 Restaurant Rush
2	
3	A turn-based restaurant management game for the command line, written in Java for the SC2002 group project.
4	The player is the manager: staff time is limited, so the manager decides which customer or order comes first.
5	
6	Repository: https://github.com/RobotHanzo/SC2002GroupProject
7	
8	## Build and run
9	
10	Needs JDK 17 or newer. No other dependencies, no network. Run every command from the repository root.
11	
12	```
13	javac --release 17 -d out -sourcepath src src/restaurantrush/Main.java
14	java -cp out restaurantrush.Main
15	```
16	
17	Replay a recorded run instead of typing the choices (the file is read and echoed, so the screen shows a transcript):
18	
19	```
20	java -cp out restaurantrush.Main --input runs/victory.txt
21	java -cp out restaurantrush.Main --input runs/defeat.txt
22	```
23	
24	Run the automated checks (see [docs/test-notes.md](docs/test-notes.md) for what they cover and for the expected results of both runs):
25	
26	```
27	javac --release 17 -d out -sourcepath src test/restaurantrush/GameTests.java
28	java -cp out restaurantrush.GameTests
29	```
30	
31	## Stage tags
32	
33	Each stage is a separate tagged commit that builds and runs on its own. `runs/` and the tests in each tag belong to that stage, so the recorded runs of an earlier stage are in its tag (`git checkout stage-1`).
34	
35	| Tag | Adds |
36	|---|---|
37	| `stage-1` | Basic operation: Regular customers, menu, Waiter, Chef, Cashier, Table, Order, the waiting queue, the full six-step turn, abandonment, victory and defeat |
38	| `stage-2` | Customer behaviour: VIP and Critic customers next to the Regulars |
39	| `stage-3` | New role and menu policy: the Host, ComboMeal and Happy Hour |
40	
41	## How to play
42	
43	The base setting is fixed: 18 turns, 2 tables, one Waiter, one Chef, one Cashier, one Host (Stage 3), and one new customer at the start of turns 1, 4, 7, 10, 13 and 16.
44	
45	| Menu entry | Price | Preparation units |
46	|---|---|---|
47	| Salad | $9.00 | 1 |
48	| Burger | $15.00 | 1 |
49	| Pasta | $18.00 | 2 |
50	| Salad + Burger (combo) | $21.60 | 2 |
51	| Salad + Pasta (combo) | $24.30 | 3 |
52	
53	A combo is made of two existing menu items: its price is 90% of the two prices added together (rounded to the nearest cent) and its preparation units are the sum. Customers pick their own food; the manager never does.
54	
55	| # | Customer | Arrives | Loses per waiting turn | Bill | Orders |
56	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
57	| 1 | VIP | turn 1 | 5 | 10% off | Burger |
58	| 2 | Regular | turn 4 | 8 | full price | Pasta |
59	| 3 | Regular | turn 7 | 8 | full price | Salad + Burger |
60	| 4 | Critic | turn 10 | 12 | full price | Salad + Pasta (the most expensive entry); loses a further 10 satisfaction if served more than 2 turns after the order was taken |
61	| 5 | Regular | turn 13 | 8 | full price | Salad |
62	| 6 | Regular | turn 16 | 8 | full price | Burger |
63	
64	Every turn the screen shows the turn number, revenue, the average satisfaction of paid customers, Happy Hour (and its remaining duration), the waiting queue, the tables, every customer's type, satisfaction and order status, and the tasks each staff member can do. Then you decide:
65	
66	| Prompt | Commands | What happens |
67	|---|---|---|
68	| Happy Hour (only while unused) | `y`, `n` (or Enter) | `y` switches Happy Hour on for this turn and the next; it can be used once per game |
69	| Host (one task per turn) | `SEAT <#>`, `WAIT` | seat a queued customer at a free table, in addition to the Waiter's task |
70	| Waiter (one task per turn) | `SEAT <#>`, `TAKE_ORDER <#>`, `SERVE <#>`, `WAIT` | seat a queued customer, take a seated customer's order, or serve a READY order |
71	| Chef (one unit per turn) | `COOK <#>`, `WAIT` | advance a customer's order by one preparation unit |
72	
73	`<#>` is the customer number shown on screen (`#2` and `2` both work). It may be left out when only one customer qualifies. A blank line means `WAIT`. A choice that cannot be done is explained and you choose again; it does not use up the staff task.
74	
75	After your choices the turn finishes on its own: customers who were served earlier finish eating (READY_TO_PAY), the Cashier charges at most one customer who became READY_TO_PAY on an earlier turn, and every customer who is still waiting for service loses satisfaction (the amount depends on the customer type). A customer who reaches 0 leaves without paying, the order is cancelled and the table is freed.
76	
77	After turn 18 you win if at least 4 customers have paid, revenue is at least $45.00 and the average satisfaction of the customers who paid is at least 60. Otherwise you lose. The game never ends early.
78	
79	### Happy Hour
80	
81	Switched on at the start of a turn, it lasts for that turn and the next, and the screen shows the turns left. While it is on:
82	
83	- orders taken get 20% off their bill. The VIP discount is applied first, then Happy Hour: a VIP's Burger is $15.00 x 0.9 x 0.8 = $10.80; a Regular's Pasta is $14.40;
84	- at the start of each active turn, customers still waiting for a table, to order, or for food recover 15 satisfaction (never above 100).
85	
86	The bill is locked when the order is taken, so Happy Hour never changes an order already placed and never touches customers who have left.
87	
88	### Rules this implementation fixes where the brief leaves a choice
89	
90	- **Food choice.** Regular and VIP customers take menu entry number `customerId mod 5` (the menu has five entries: Salad 0, Burger 1, Pasta 2, Salad + Burger 3, Salad + Pasta 4), so customer 1 orders a Burger, 2 a Pasta, 3 the Salad + Burger combo, 4 the Salad + Pasta combo, 5 a Salad. The Critic ignores that rule and takes the most expensive entry. The choice is shown when the order is taken.
91	- **Price lock.** The bill is worked out when the Waiter takes the order (customer's own pricing first, then Happy Hour) and is stored in the Order; the Cashier collects that amount.
92	- **Critic reaction.** "Served more than 2 turns after the order was taken" means the serving turn minus the order turn is 3 or more. A 3-unit combo ordered on turn *n* is READY at the end of turn *n*+2 at the earliest and served on turn *n*+3, so the Critic's combo always costs the extra 10 satisfaction; with a 2-unit dish a prompt Waiter avoids it.
93	- **Order of events in a turn.** Arrival, Happy Hour choice and recovery, your Host choice, your Waiter choice, your Chef choice (so the Chef can work on an order taken this turn, and the Waiter can take the order of a customer the Host has just seated), eating and payment, then waiting and abandonment. A dish the Chef finishes this turn is READY only after the Waiter has acted, so it is served next turn at the earliest.
94	- **Eating and payment.** A customer served on turn *n* is READY_TO_PAY on turn *n*+1 and can pay on turn *n*+2. "Oldest first" means the customer who arrived first.
95	
96	## From stage-1 to stage-2
97	
98	Compare: https://github.com/RobotHanzo/SC2002GroupProject/compare/stage-1...stage-2
99	
100	The Stage 2 commits are `efec627`, `69a34cb`, `bc1cd9f` and `10f4b5e` plus the diagram and documentation commits after them.
101	
102	**New files**
103	
104	| File | Responsibility |
105	|---|---|
106	| `entity/Menu.java` | Owns the dishes and finds the most expensive one (the Critic needs it) |
107	| `entity/RegularCustomer.java` | Baseline customer: loses 8, full price |
108	| `entity/VIPCustomer.java` | Loses 5, bill is 90% of the list price |
109	| `entity/CriticCustomer.java` | Loses 12, orders the most expensive dish, upset by slow service |
110	| `control/ArrivalSchedule.java` | Creates the six base-setting arrivals and decides their type (VIP on turn 1, Critic on turn 10) |
111	
112	**Existing files changed, and why**
113	
114	| File | Change | Reason |
115	|---|---|---|
116	| `entity/Customer.java` | Became abstract; added the hooks `getTypeLabel`, `getSatisfactionLoss`, `calculateBillCents`, `onServed` and `adjustSatisfaction`; `chooseMenuItem` takes a `Menu`; the fixed loss of 8 moved to `RegularCustomer` | Customer types must differ without the controller testing the type. All turn guards (`canFinishEating`, `canPay`) and the status rules are untouched |
117	| `entity/Order.java` | Constructor takes the bill and the turn it was taken; `getPriceCents()` became `getBillCents()` | The VIP discount means the price is no longer the item's list price, so the bill is locked in the order when it is taken. The status transitions are untouched |
118	| `entity/Waiter.java` | `takeOrder` takes a `Menu` and the turn, asks the customer for the dish and the bill, and returns the `Order`; `serveOrder` calls `customer.onServed` | This is where type-specific behaviour is dispatched: the Waiter calls the methods on `Customer` and never checks the type |
119	| `entity/Cashier.java` | Reads `getBillCents()` | Follows the Order change; one line |
120	| `entity/Restaurant.java` | Holds a `Menu` instead of a list; `registerArrival(Customer)` receives the customer instead of creating one | The schedule now decides which kind of customer arrives |
121	| `control/GameController.java` | Holds an `ArrivalSchedule` and asks it for arrivals; passes the `Menu` and turn to the Waiter; messages show the customer type | Registration of the new classes. The six-step turn, the guards and the result rule are unchanged |
122	| `boundary/RestaurantCLI.java` | Shows the type, and the locked bill of each order | Display only |
123	| `runs/victory.txt`, `runs/defeat.txt` | Replaced | The base-setting arrivals now include a VIP and a Critic, so the Stage 1 inputs no longer fit. The Stage 1 runs are still in the `stage-1` tag |
124	| `test/restaurantrush/GameTests.java` | Extended | See below |
125	
126	**Unchanged:** `Chef`, `Table`, `MenuItem`, `CustomerStatus`, `OrderStatus`, `Money`, `Main`.
127	
128	**Deleted:** nothing.
129	
130	**What still works.** `GameTests` keeps the Stage 1 checks that still apply (menu, order status rules, the Regular's loss of 8, the turn guards for eating and payment, the Table, payment and abandonment), rewritten only where a constructor changed, and adds the Stage 2 checks. It also checks that `GameController` contains no `instanceof` and never names a customer type.
131	
132	## From stage-2 to stage-3
133	
134	Compare: https://github.com/RobotHanzo/SC2002GroupProject/compare/stage-2...stage-3
135	
136	The Stage 3 feature commits are `d6fbec7`, `0231d21`, `9ed8d12`, `9225d1b`, `7d210d3` and `a6e64d3`. They are followed by the diagram and documentation commits and by one clean-up commit, `74c50eb`, with its diagram commit `ba84062` (see "Stage 3 clean-up" below).
137	
138	**New files**
139	
140	| File | Responsibility |
141	|---|---|
142	| `entity/ComboMeal.java` | A menu item made of two existing menu items (composition); computes the 90% price and the summed preparation units |
143	| `entity/HappyHour.java` | The pricing policy: once per game, two turns, 20% off new orders, +15 satisfaction for waiting customers |
144	| `entity/Host.java` | The new staff role: seats one queued customer per turn on top of the Waiter's task |
145	| `control/ManagerCommand.java` | Clean-up: reads one typed manager choice (a verb and an optional customer number) and picks the customer it refers to |
146	| `control/StaffActions.java` | Clean-up: carries out a Host, Waiter or Chef command and returns the sentence to show; an impossible command throws before anything changes |
147	
148	**Existing files changed, and why**
149	
150	| File | Change | Reason |
151	|---|---|---|
152	| `entity/MenuItem.java` | No longer `final` (one word) | `ComboMeal` extends it, so Waiter, Chef, Cashier, Menu and Order treat a combo like any dish and need no change |
153	| `entity/Customer.java` | Added `recoverSatisfaction`; `isAwaitingService()` became public; clean-up: added `getDisplayName()` | Happy Hour recovery and the controller select customers who are still waiting. The existing satisfaction clamp (`adjustSatisfaction`) is reused. `getDisplayName()` gives every message one way to name a customer |
154	| `entity/Waiter.java` | `takeOrder` also takes the `HappyHour` and applies it after the customer's own price | Happy Hour is applied after the VIP discount and locked in the Order. Seating, serving and the Critic hook are untouched |
155	| `entity/Restaurant.java` | Owns a `Host` and a `HappyHour` (with getters); the menu now lists two combos. Clean-up: `getWaitingQueue()` is read-only and `seatFromQueue` is the only way out of the queue; `getCustomersWithStatus`, `getSeatableCustomers`, `getCustomersToServe` and `getCustomersToCook` say what each staff member can do now; the three victory targets and `hasMetObjective()` | Registration of the new classes and of the combos, once, at set-up. The clean-up gives the queue and the staff-task rules one owner: the controller used to remove customers from the live queue itself, and the screen and the controller each worked out what a staff member can do. Payment and abandonment logic is untouched |
156	| `entity/Order.java` | Clean-up: removed the unused `orderId` and its static counter | Nothing read the id, and a static counter is global state shared by every game. Status rules and the locked bill are untouched |
157	| `control/GameController.java` | New steps before the staff choices: Happy Hour activation and recovery, and the Host choice; `HappyHour.endTurn()` at the end of the turn; the waiting loop uses `isAwaitingService()`. Clean-up: command parsing and task execution moved to `ManagerCommand` and `StaffActions`; the result check moved to `Restaurant.hasMetObjective()` | The Stage 3 turn needs the new steps. The clean-up leaves the controller sequencing the six steps (324 to 150 lines). The Waiter and Chef logic, payment and result rule are unchanged |
158	| `boundary/RestaurantCLI.java` | Host and Happy Hour prompts, Happy Hour status line, Host task line. Clean-up: no longer references `GameController`; the turn number and targets are passed in; the task lines print what `Restaurant` reports | Display and input only. Boundary and control no longer depend on each other |
159	| `Main.java` | Clean-up: creates the CLI and the controller (`Main.play`) and handles the end of the input | So that `RestaurantCLI` does not need the controller |
160	| `runs/victory.txt`, `runs/defeat.txt` | Replaced | There are two more prompts (Happy Hour and Host), and the menu changes what customers order. The Stage 2 runs are in the `stage-2` tag |
161	| `test/restaurantrush/GameTests.java` | Extended | See below |
162	
163	**Unchanged:** `Menu`, `Table`, `Chef`, `Cashier`, `ArrivalSchedule`, `RegularCustomer`, `VIPCustomer`, `CriticCustomer`, the enums, `Money`.
164	
165	**Deleted:** nothing.
166	
167	**Stage 3 clean-up (no behaviour change).** After the Stage 3 features worked, review found structure the features did not need but the design should not keep: a controller that also parsed commands and picked customers, the screen repeating the staff-task rules, a live queue handed out for the controller to edit, a static order counter, and a controller and screen that referenced each other. The clean-up commit `74c50eb` fixes these in the files listed above and touches no rule: the Waiter, Host, Chef, Cashier, `HappyHour`, `ComboMeal` and the customer types are unchanged. Evidence that behaviour is the same: `runs/victory.txt`, `runs/defeat.txt` and an invalid-input session print byte-identical transcripts before and after, and every earlier check still passes.
168	
169	**What still works.** `GameTests` keeps every Stage 1 and Stage 2 check (the Stage 1 checks that used the three-dish menu now build that menu explicitly, because the game menu has five entries) and adds the combo reference values, Happy Hour timing, discount, price lock and recovery, the Host, and (clean-up) the queue protection, staff task lists and objective check. The recorded runs replay the whole game. See [docs/test-notes.md](docs/test-notes.md).

2	SC2002 Restaurant Rush Group Assignment
3	Java command-line turn-based restaurant management game  |  Student brief
4	Your group builds a playable restaurant management game in Java. The player is the manager. Staff time is limited, so the manager decides which customers and orders come first. You build the game in three stages, and each stage must show how your object-oriented design supports the next set of requirements.
5	Item

21	Implement a complete game with meaningful player choices, a clear result and robust input handling.
22	2  Game setup
23	2.1  Base setting

53	2.2  General rules
54	Customers choose their own food. The manager never chooses it. Use a documented deterministic rule or seeded randomness, and show the choice once the order is placed.
55	Keep rules out of the UI. The UI only collects the manager’s decisions and displays the state. Game rules belong in domain and control objects.
56	Money. If fractional prices arise, store amounts as integer cents. Display dollars with two decimals.
57	Offline. All mandatory features must work without network access.
58	3  How a turn works
59	3.1  Start-of-turn display
60	At the start of every turn, show: the turn number, revenue, average satisfaction of paid customers, the waiting queue, occupied tables, each customer’s satisfaction, order status, and the staff tasks available.
61	3.2  Turn steps
62	Each turn then runs these six steps in order.
63	Step
64	What happens
65	1  Arrivals
66	The scheduled customer joins the waiting queue with satisfaction 100.
67	2  Manager choices
68	Waiter: choose at most one task, or Wait: seat one queued customer at a free table, take one seated customer’s order, or serve one READY order.
69	Chef: choose at most one order to advance by one preparation unit, or Wait. The Chef may work on an order the Waiter took this turn.
70	3  Kitchen and serving
71	The Waiter acts before the Chef. When an order’s last preparation unit is done, it becomes READY. A dish can be served only if it was already READY when the Waiter acts, so a dish finished this turn is served next turn at the earliest. Serving sets it to SERVED.
72	4  Eating and payment
73	A customer served on an earlier turn finishes one full turn of eating and becomes READY_TO_PAY. The Cashier automatically handles at most one READY_TO_PAY customer per turn, oldest first. A customer who became READY_TO_PAY this turn pays next turn at the earliest. Payment adds the bill to revenue, marks the customer and order PAID, and frees the table.
74	5  Waiting and abandonment
75	At turn end, every waiting or seated customer who has not been served loses satisfaction (Regular: 8 points, minimum 0). A customer who reaches 0 leaves without paying: cancel any unfinished order and free the table. Customers who are served, eating or ready to pay lose nothing.
76	6  Result
77	The game always runs all 18 turns; there is no early ending. After turn 18: Victory if all three targets in Section 2.1 are met, otherwise Defeat.
78	
79	3.3  Example: one customer from arrival to payment
80	This example follows one Regular customer who orders Pasta (2 preparation units) and gets the manager’s full attention.

82	What happens
83	Satisfaction at turn end

85	Arrives (100). Waiter seats the customer.
86	92 (seated, not served)
87	2
88	Waiter takes the Pasta order. Chef does unit 1 of 2.
89	84
90	3
91	Chef does unit 2 of 2: order is READY. The Waiter cannot serve it this turn.
92	76
93	4
94	Waiter serves: SERVED.
95	76 (no further loss)
96	5
97	Customer finishes eating: READY_TO_PAY.
98	76
99	6
100	Cashier collects $18.00: PAID, table freed. Satisfaction 76 is recorded.
101	—
102	
103	3.4  Rules that always apply
104	A Waiter completes at most one task per turn.
105	A table stays occupied until its customer pays or leaves.
106	A customer cannot eat before being served. The Cashier cannot charge before READY_TO_PAY.
107	An invalid choice must explain why it failed and let the manager choose again, without using up the staff task.
108	Revenue never includes unpaid or abandoned orders.
109	The customer who arrives on turn 16 cannot finish paying within 18 turns, so at most five customers can pay. This is intended, not a bug.
110	3.5  End-of-turn results
111	Show the served count, unhappy departures, revenue, average satisfaction of paid customers, and turns used. The average uses the satisfaction recorded when each customer pays. If no one has paid yet, show N/A.

113	Each stage is a separate runnable version tagged in GitHub, and each builds on the one before.

154	All customer types follow the same order and service flow.

157	4.3  Stage 3: New role and menu policy
158	Host. The Host seats at most one queued customer per turn, in addition to the Waiter’s task. The Waiter can still seat customers.
159	ComboMeal. A combo is made of two existing menu items.
160	Preparation units = the sum of its two components.
161	Price = 90% of the components’ combined price, rounded to the nearest cent. Example: Salad + Pasta = ($9 + $18) × 0.9 = $24.30, 3 units.
162	A customer chooses a combo according to a rule you document.
163	Happy Hour. A pricing policy the manager may switch on.
164	Can be activated once per game, at the start of a turn. It lasts for that turn and the next. Display its remaining duration.
165	Orders taken while it is active get 20% off their bill.
166	During each active turn, customers still waiting for a table or food recover 15 satisfaction at turn start (maximum 100).
167	Apply the VIP discount first, then Happy Hour. The price is locked when the order is taken. Example: a VIP orders a Burger during Happy Hour: $15 × 0.9 × 0.8 = $10.80.
168	It does not change orders already placed, and does not affect customers who have left.
169	Stage 3 is independently playable.

1	                 SC2002 Restaurant Rush: Group Report
2	
3	                 SCEC (Group 5)
4	
5	1. Introduction
6	
7	Restaurant Rush is a turn-based restaurant game played in the terminal. The player is the manager. Each
8	turn the Waiter, Chef and (from Stage 3) Host can each do one task, so the manager has to decide which
9	customer or order gets attention first. We built it in three stages, each extending the saved version of
10	the previous one, and tagged each stage as its own runnable commit.
11	
12	Base setting.
13	
14	18 turns, 2 tables, one Waiter, one Chef, one Cashier, one Host (Stage 3). One customer arrives at the
15	start of turns 1, 4, 7, 10, 13 and 16.
16	
17	After turn 18 the player wins with at least 4 paid customers, revenue of at least $45.00 and an average
18	satisfaction of at least 60 among paying customers. The game never ends early.
19	
20	The code is in the GitHub repository, with one tag per stage (stage-1, stage-2, stage-3). The changes
21	between stages can be compared at stage-1...stage-2 and stage-2...stage-3.
22	
23	The diagrams are in docs/diagrams/ (PlantUML sources in docs/diagrams/src/), and docs/test-
24	notes.md lists the expected test and run results for each stage.
25	
26	How to run (JDK 17+, from the repository root, no other dependencies):
27	
28	javac --release 17 -d out -sourcepath src src/restaurantrush/Main.java
29	java -cp out restaurantrush.Main
30	java -cp out restaurantrush.Main --input runs/victory.txt
31	java -cp out restaurantrush.Main --input runs/defeat.txt
32	
33	The --input option replays a recorded set of manager choices and echoes them, so both endings can be
34	
35	seen without typing anything.
36	
37	Stage snapshots. Each tag builds from a clean checkout and carries its own runs/ and tests. The Java
38	source at stage-1 has no customer subclasses, Host, ComboMeal or HappyHour, and stage-2 has none
39	of the Stage 3 classes.
40	
41	The Stage 2 feature commits are efec627, 69a34cb, bc1cd9f and 10f4b5e.
42	
43	The Stage 3 feature commits are d6fbec7, 0231d21, 9ed8d12, 9225d1b, 7d210d3 and a6e64d3, followed
44	by the clean-up 74c50eb.
45

47	appear in the stage-1 tag; the source code itself follows the stage boundaries.

67	2. Stage 1: the baseline game
68	
69	Stage 1 is a complete game with Regular customers only, and we wrote it as if Stages 2 and 3 did not
70	exist. The tag has no customer subclasses, no Host, no combo and no Happy Hour.
71	
72	We split the classes along the entity-control-boundary lines from the lectures:
73	
74	Layer    Class                   Responsibility
75	
76	Boundary RestaurantCLI           Prints the turn status and reads the manager's
77	                                 choices
78	
79	Control  GameController          Runs the 18 turns and the fixed order of steps inside
80	                                 a turn
81	
82	Entity   Restaurant              Owns the tables, staff, menu, customers and the
83	                                 waiting queue; records revenue
84	
85	Entity   Waiter, Chef, Cashier   One staff task each: seat/take order/serve, advance
86	                                 preparation, take payment
87	
88	Entity   Customer                Satisfaction, status and the turn guards
89	                                 (canFinishEating, canPay, shouldAbandon)
90	
91	Entity   Order                   Preparation progress and the order status
92	                                 transitions
93	
94	Entity   Table, MenuItem         A seat with at most one occupant; a dish with a
95	                                 price and preparation units
96	
97	Utility  Money, CustomerStatus,  Cents formatting and the two state enums
98	
99	         OrderStatus
100	
101	Turn order. 1 arrivals, 2-3 manager choices for the Waiter and Chef, 4 eating and payment, 5 waiting
102	and abandonment, 6 result after turn 18. The Waiter acts before the Chef, so a dish the Chef finishes
103	this turn can only be served next turn. A customer served on turn n becomes READY_TO_PAY on turn
104	n+1 and can pay on turn n+2.
105	
106	State protection. Order keeps its status private. It moves only through advancePreparation(),
107	markServed(), markPaid() and cancel(), and an illegal move throws.
108	
109	The legal path is PLACED  PREPARING  READY  SERVED  PAID, with PLACED  READY for a one-
110	unit dish and CANCELLED when a customer leaves. Customer never lets satisfaction fall below 0 and
111	checks its own status before every transition. Because these checks sit next to the state, an invalid
112	manager command cannot half-change the game.
113	
114	Money is stored in integer cents throughout, so a discount such as 10% then 20% off always gives the
115	same amount, rounded to the cent, instead of a floating-point rounding error.
116	
117	The Stage 1 class diagram is docs/diagrams/stage-1-class.pdf and is shown in the appendix.
118	
119	3. Stage 1  Stage 2: customer behaviour
120	
121	Stage 2 adds VIP and Critic customers. We made Customer abstract and let each subclass override the
122	behaviour that differs, so no other class has to check which type of customer it is dealing with.
123	
124	Type              Loss per Bill         Food choice               Reaction to service
125	
126	                  waiting

135	                           price

138	                                                                  2 turns after ordering
139	
140	The varying behaviour sits behind four hooks on Customer:
141	
142	getSatisfactionLoss(), calculateBillCents(), chooseMenuItem(Menu) and
143	onServed(turnsWaited).
144	
145	Waiter.takeOrder calls chooseMenuItem and calculateBillCents, and serveOrder calls onServed,
146	all through a Customer reference; the end-of-turn loss goes through reduceSatisfaction(), which
147	calls getSatisfactionLoss(). Dynamic dispatch picks the right version each time. The controller
148	contains no instanceof, and GameTests checks that by scanning the source.

203	Effect on the design. Several files changed, but each change was small. Customer gained an abstract
204	contract, and every other edit is a signature change that follows from the bill being locked into Order.
205	Adding a fourth customer type now means one new subclass and one line in ArrivalSchedule.
206	4. Stage 2  Stage 3: Host, combo meals and Happy Hour
207	
208	Stage 3 adds a new staff role, a two-item combo and a once-per-game pricing policy. Most of the change
209	is new classes plus registration in Restaurant and GameController. The order, payment and customer
210	logic from Stage 2 was left alone.
211	
212	Host. Host.seatCustomer(customer, table) seats one queued customer per turn on top of the
213	Waiter's task. It reuses Table.seat and Customer.markSeated, so the same guards apply: an occupied
214	table or a customer who is not WAITING is refused and nothing changes.
215	
216	ComboMeal (composition). ComboMeal extends MenuItem and holds final references to two existing
217	MenuItems. Its price is 90% of their sum, rounded to the nearest cent, and its preparation units are the
218	sum. Because it is a MenuItem, Waiter, Chef, Cashier, Menu and Order need no changes to handle a
219	combo. The only edit needed was removing final from MenuItem.
220	
221	With the two combos the menu has five entries: Salad ($9.00, 1 unit), Burger ($15.00, 1 unit), Pasta
222	($18.00, 2 units), Salad + Burger ($21.60, 2 units) and Salad + Pasta ($24.30, 3 units). The combo values
223	match the reference values in section 7.
224	
225	HappyHour (policy). It can be switched on once, at the start of a turn, and lasts that turn and the next.
226	While active, new orders get 20% off and customers still waiting for a table, an order or their food
227	recover 15 satisfaction (capped at 100). Waiter.takeOrder applies it after the customer's own pricing
228	and stores the result in Order, so a VIP Burger is $15.00 � 0.9 � 0.8 = $10.80. The price is locked at that
229	moment and is never recalculated, so Happy Hour ending before payment does not change any bill.
230

285	logic that already worked, so we kept the refactor in one separate commit (74c50eb) after the Stage 3
286	features already worked. It moved code without changing any rule. The evidence is that the victory,
287	defeat and invalid-input sessions print byte-identical transcripts before and after it, and all earlier checks
288	still pass. We did it because the controller had grown to parse input, choose customers, run staff tasks
289	and format messages. In other words, one class had four separate reasons to change.

349	5. Sequence diagram: a VIP orders a Burger during Happy Hour
350	
351	We picked a Stage 3 use case so that one scenario would show a Stage 2 behaviour (the VIP discount), a
352	Stage 3 feature (Happy Hour) and the whole flow from arrival to payment. The diagram is
353	docs/diagrams/stage-3-sequence.pdf (three pages, source stage-3-sequence.puml).
354	
355	Stage                  Stage 3, base setting (18 turns, 2 tables)
356	Starting conditions        Turn 1, restaurant empty, both tables free, Happy Hour unused. The
357	                           VIP arrives with satisfaction 100
358	
359	Manager decision           Switch Happy Hour on in turn 1 (y) or not (n)
360	
361	Outcome with y             Bill locked at $10.80; VIP pays on turn 5 with satisfaction 95
362	
363	Outcome with n             Bill $13.50; satisfaction 90 at payment
364	
365	Lifelines                  Manager (actor), main : Main, cli : RestaurantCLI (boundary),
366	                           game : GameController, staff : StaffActions, cmd :
367	                           ManagerCommand (control), schedule, restaurant, hh :
368	                           HappyHour, t1 : Table, host, waiter, chef, cashier, vip :
369	                           VIPCustomer, order : Order (entity)
370	
371	Walkthrough, turn by turn
372	
373	1. Turn 1. game asks schedule.isArrivalTurn(1), then createArrival(1) creates the
374	    VIPCustomer (WAITING), which restaurant.registerArrival adds to the queue. game checks
375	    hh.isAvailable() and cli asks the manager; on y, hh.activate() sets two remaining turns
376	    (an alt shows the n branch). The Host is left on WAIT, so the Waiter seats the VIP: cmd =
377	    parse(choice), staff.waiter(cmd, 1), then restaurant.seatFromQueue(vip, t1,
378	    waiter::seatCustomer), which calls waiter.seatCustomer(vip, t1); the Waiter then calls
379	    t1.seat(vip) and vip.markSeated(). The queue entry is removed only after seating
380	    succeeds. At the end of the turn the waiting loop calls vip.reduceSatisfaction(), which
381	    dispatches to the VIP's loss of 5 (100  95).
382	
383	2. Turn 2. Happy Hour recovery gives +15 to waiting customers (95  100). The Waiter takes the
384	    order: the VIP chooses the Burger, calculateBillCents returns $13.50 (VIP override),
385	    hh.applyDiscount makes it $10.80, and new Order(item, bill, turn) locks it. The Chef
386	    then calls order.advancePreparation(); one unit is enough, so the order goes PLACED 
387	    READY. The Waiter has already acted this turn, so serving waits for turn 3. End of turn: 100 
388	    95.
389	
390	3. Turn 3. The Waiter serves inside a loop until the choice is accepted. An alt shows the
391	    guard: a READY order is served (markServed, VIP  SERVED, servedTurn = 3); otherwise cmd
392	    throws "only a customer with a READY order can be served" and the manager is asked again
393	    without losing the task.
394	
395	4. Turn 4. Customer 2 arrives and the Host seats them. In step 4, vip.canFinishEating(4) is
396	    true, so the VIP becomes READY_TO_PAY (readyTurn = 4).
397	
398	5. Turn 5. restaurant finds the oldest eligible payer, cashier.processPayment(vip,
399	    restaurant) reads the locked getBillCents() (not recalculated), recordPayment adds $10.80
400	    to revenue, the VIP and order become PAID, and releaseTableOf(vip) frees t1.
401	
402	6. After turn 18. restaurant.hasMetObjective() checks paid count, revenue and average
403	    satisfaction, and an alt shows victory or defeat.
404	
405	How the interactions implement the use case. The messages show two of our design decisions. The
406	controller never checks what type of customer it has; the VIP's smaller loss and its discount come from
407	overridden methods called on a Customer reference. The bill is also worked out only once, in
408	Waiter.takeOrder, and stored in Order. On turn 5 the Cashier just reads that amount, which is how
409	the price stays locked from the moment the order is taken.
410	
411	6. Design considerations
412	
413	6.1 Who owns what
414	
415	We tried to give every rule one clear owner. GameController only decides the order of the steps in a
416	turn; at 150 lines it no longer parses input or runs tasks itself. ManagerCommand reads what the manager
417	typed and works out which customer it refers to, and StaffActions turns that command into a call on
418	the Host, Waiter or Chef. Anything that differs between customer types lives in the Customer
419	subclasses. Billing is split on purpose: Waiter.takeOrder works out the price, Order keeps it and
420	Cashier only reads it. Restaurant owns the queue, the tables and the rules for who each staff member
421	can act on, so the screen and the controller always agree. RestaurantCLI only displays and reads input.
422	
423	Restaurant is the largest entity at about 230 lines, but most of that is small query methods over data it
424	already owns, so no single class ends up doing everything.
425	
426	6.2 OO concepts and SOLID, with code evidence
427	
428	    � Single Responsibility. The Stage 3 clean-up split the old controller into GameController,
429	         ManagerCommand and StaffActions, which took it from 324 to 150 lines.
430	
431	    � Open/Closed. Stage 2 added three customer types without editing the controller's turn logic.
432	         When Stage 3 added ComboMeal, Chef, Cashier, Menu and Order needed no change to handle
433	         it.
434	
435	    � Liskov Substitution. Any Customer subclass can be used wherever a Customer is expected, and
436	         the shared lifecycle guards still hold. A ComboMeal works anywhere a MenuItem does; a test puts
437	         a 3-unit combo in an ordinary Order and checks it is READY only after the third
438	         advancePreparation(), the same call the Chef makes.
439	
440	    � Dependency direction. The CLI no longer depends on the controller (Stage 3 clean-up). Main
441	         wires them together, and GameTests checks that the CLI source never mentions
442	         GameController.
443	
444	    � Encapsulation. Order and customer state change only through methods that check the current
445	         status. getWaitingQueue() returns a read-only view and seatFromQueue is the only way out of
446	         the queue.
447	
448	    � Polymorphism vs instanceof. GameTests reads the controller, StaffActions and
449	         ManagerCommand sources and fails if any of them contains instanceof or names a customer
450	         type.
451	
452	6.3 Trade-offs and alternatives we considered
453

457	a concrete    interface with     interface would add indirection  concrete class. A second
458	class         Happy Hour as one  without a real second variant    promotion would mean
459	Decision       Alternative           Why we chose ours                     Consequence
