592	
593	8. Individual reflections
594	
595	8.1 Murugesan Peramudhan
596	
597	1. My Contribution
598	
599	My main responsibility was the Stage 3 sequence diagram. I chose the use case of a VIP customer
600	ordering a Burger while Happy Hour is active, because it combines a Stage 2 behaviour (the VIP
601	discount) with a Stage 3 feature (Happy Hour) and goes through the full food and payment flow within
602	five turns.
603	
604	I wrote the first complete version of the diagram in PlantUML. It covered the customer's arrival, the
605	Happy Hour choice and recovery, the Waiter's choices, the Chef preparing the order, eating, payment
606	and the final result check. I also worked out the numbers for the scenario note, such as the locked bill of
607	$15.00 x 0.9 x 0.8 = $10.80 and the different outcome if Happy Hour is not used ($13.50 and satisfaction
608	90), and checked them against the values in our tests.
609	
610	Besides drafting the diagram, I exported it as SVG and PNG so the group could review it. After the Stage
611	3 clean-up changed parts of the code, Mu-En reworked the diagram into the final three-page version,
612	and parts of my draft were carried over into it.
613	2. What I Learned
614	
615	Before this project, I mainly saw sequence diagrams as a way to show the order in which things happen.
616	While working on the Stage 3 diagram, I realised that each arrow is really a statement about which
617	object is responsible for an action. Deciding who calls whom was more challenging than simply listing
618	the steps.
619	
620	One concept that became clearer to me was polymorphism. When I traced how the VIP's bill is
621	calculated, I saw that the Waiter calls calculateBillCents() on the customer, and the VIPCustomer class
622	applies its own 10% discount. The controller does not need to check what type of customer it is.
623	Drawing this message going directly to the VIP object helped me understand polymorphism better than
624	just reading the definition in lectures.
625	
626	Polymorphism also made me understand inheritance and abstraction better. Customer is an abstract
627	class, so the game can never create a plain "Customer"; it has to be a RegularCustomer, VIPCustomer or
628	CriticCustomer. The shared lifecycle (WAITING, SEATED, ORDERED and so on) is written once in
629	Customer, while each subclass overrides only what is different, such as getSatisfactionLoss() returning 8,
630	5 or 12. In lectures I thought of inheritance mainly as a way to reuse code. In this project I saw that its
631	bigger purpose is to give every customer the same contract, so other classes can treat them all the same
632	way.
633	
634	I also learned that encapsulation is about protecting rules, not just making fields private. In Order, the
635	status can only change through methods like advancePreparation(), markServed() and markPaid(), and
636	an illegal change, such as paying for an order that was never served, throws an exception. The bill is set
637	once in the constructor and has no setter, which is why the price cannot change after the order is taken.
638	When I drew the sequence diagram, this meant every state change had to be a message to the object
639	that owns that state, rather than another class changing it directly.
640	
641	Another concept I understood better was the difference between inheritance and composition.
642	ComboMeal uses both: it extends MenuItem, so a combo can be used anywhere a normal dish can, and
643	it also holds two existing MenuItems, which is where its price and preparation units come from.
644	Restaurant, on the other hand, uses composition for its tables and staff, which it creates and owns, and
645	aggregation for customers, who arrive and leave. Before this project I found it hard to tell these
646	relationships apart, but seeing them in our own classes made the difference clearer.
647	
648	Finally, the project showed me why the Open/Closed Principle matters. When the VIP and Critic
649	customers were added in Stage 2, the controller's turn logic did not need to change, and when
650	ComboMeal was added in Stage 3, the Chef, Cashier and Order classes did not need any change to
651	handle combos. Our tests even check that the controller never uses instanceof or mentions a customer
652	type. This helped me see that good use of abstraction and polymorphism is what lets a program grow
653	without rewriting parts that already work.
654	
655	I also gained a better understanding of UML control notation. A loop, opt or alt fragment is not just
656	decoration; each one should match a loop or condition that actually exists in the code. If a guard is in the
657	code but missing from the diagram, the diagram is not accurate.
658	
659	3. How I Applied What I Learned
660	When drawing the diagram, I tried to show the price locking rule clearly. In our design, the price is
661	locked when the order is taken. In my diagram, the Waiter creates the Order with the bill already
662	calculated, and on turn 5 the Cashier only reads the stored amount instead of calculating the price
663	again. This showed why the Cashier does not need to know about customer types or Happy Hour, which
664	is an example of the Single Responsibility Principle.
665	
666	I also applied the game's turn rules to place each message on the correct turn. The Chef finishes the
667	Burger on turn 2, but since the Waiter has already acted that turn, the order can only be served on turn
668	3. The customer then finishes eating on turn 4 and pays on turn 5. I went through the turn order in the
669	README step by step to make sure the timing in the diagram was correct.
670	
671	4. Challenges and Solutions
672	
673	One challenge was keeping the diagram consistent with the code while the code was still changing. I
674	drew my version before the Stage 3 clean-up, which moved command handling into ManagerCommand
675	and StaffActions and changed how customers are removed from the waiting queue. After that, my
676	diagram no longer matched the code, even though it was correct when I first drew it. I realised that a
677	diagram that disagrees with the code is not useful, so the final version had to be redrawn based on the
678	updated classes. From this, I learned that the final sequence diagram should be completed only after the
679	code for that stage is stable.
680	
681	Another challenge was readability. My first version showed one general turn inside a large loop, with an
682	alt fragment for each of the Waiter's choices. Although it was compact, it was difficult to follow which
683	branch happened on which turn, and the diagram became very large. The final version solved this by
684	showing each turn separately across three pages, which made the flow much easier to read.
685	
686	I also had some difficulty with the PlantUML tool. The online renderer ignored some of the formatting
687	settings I used and printed a warning box onto the exported image. I replaced those settings with a style
688	block, rendered the diagram again and checked the exported image before sharing it with my group.
689	
690	5. Key Takeaway
691	
692	Overall, this project helped me understand how object-oriented concepts such as polymorphism,
693	encapsulation and the Single Responsibility Principle appear in the interactions between objects, not
694	just in the class structure. I learned that a sequence diagram is only useful if it accurately reflects the
695	code and the rules of the system. I also realised that it is important to coordinate with teammates when
696	the code changes, so that the diagrams and implementation stay consistent.
697	
698	8.2 Li Mu-En
699	
700	My contribution
701	
702	My main contribution was turning the group's design drafts into a consistent, runnable three-stage
703	game and taking responsibility for the design decisions carried through to the submission. This involved
704	interpreting the requirements, deciding where responsibilities belonged, evaluating proposed changes,
705	and keeping the code, diagrams and stage tags aligned.
706	    � Repository and brief (30 Oct). I reorganised the repository and added the instructors' briefing,
707	         assignment and TA rubric (f726dea). These gave us a common reference for evaluating the
708	         design and implementation.
709	
710	    � Design and diagrams (30 Oct to 3 Nov). I reworked the group's three class diagrams and Stage 3
711	         sequence diagram against the requirements. My decisions included keeping customer lifecycle
712	         guards in the domain classes, retaining a concrete HappyHour class, and correcting diagrams
713	         where the existing code had the more appropriate responsibility allocation. I maintained the
714	         PlantUML sources and PDFs and aligned each diagram's members and relationships with its
715	         stage (f62b036, a9f6457, 7998537, 983cc8a).
716	
717	    � Staged implementation and integration (3 Nov). I directed the implementation around
718	         independently runnable stage-1, stage-2 and stage-3 tags. I decided which review findings
719	         warranted changes and kept the structural clean-up within Stage 3 (74c50eb). I also put
720	         together the README comparison tables and test evidence, and addressed the SERVE and COOK
721	         shortcut bug (983cc8a).
722	
723	The central part of my contribution was deciding what to preserve, what to change, and why. For
724	example, I retained valid lifecycle logic in Customer and Restaurant when the diagrams disagreed with
725	it, but accepted refactoring when the controller had accumulated unrelated responsibilities. Both
726	decisions required considering cohesion, encapsulation and the effect on existing behaviour.
727	
728	Use of AI. I used Claude Code to help draft the Java code according to the requirements and design
729	direction I supplied. The diagrams and other documents, including the README and test notes, were
730	drafted by humans, reviewed with AI assistance, and amended by me after evaluating the feedback. I set
731	the stage boundaries and decided which suggestions to accept or reject, retaining responsibility for the
732	core design decisions, trade-offs and acceptance of the result. The linked chat histories document that
733	assistance.
734	
735	What did I learn?
736	
737	I developed a more practical understanding of OOP by asking which object should own a rule, which
738	details callers should know, and what should remain unchanged when a feature is added. These
739	questions helped me evaluate the implementation beyond whether the game ran.
740	
741	Abstraction and polymorphism give callers a stable contract. The abstract Customer class holds the
742	shared lifecycle and exposes methods for the behaviour that varies: waiting loss, menu choice, billing
743	and reaction to service. Waiter.takeOrder calls customer.chooseMenuItem(menu) and
744	customer.calculateBillCents(...) through a Customer reference. Dynamic dispatch selects the
745	appropriate behaviour. My design criterion was that adding a customer type should not require another
746	type-checking branch in the controller. The base class preserves the shared lifecycle, while subclasses
747	override the relevant behaviour. This gives inheritance a purpose beyond reusing fields.
748	
749	Encapsulation protects rules as well as fields. Private state is useful only if the available methods
750	preserve its invariants. Order controls its status transitions, while the Waiter checks readiness before
751	serving. Customer owns its lifecycle guards and keeps satisfaction within 0 to 100.
752	Restaurant.seatFromQueue removes a customer only after seating succeeds. I used these examples to
753	judge whether an invalid action could leave the game partly changed. Checks beside the state they
754	protect apply to every caller, including callers outside the CLI.
755	Cohesion and coupling affect how easily a design changes. A controller that parses input, selects
756	customers, executes tasks and formats messages has several reasons to change. Separating those
757	responsibilities made each class easier to explain and reduced the knowledge shared between the
758	screen and controller. I also learned that a UML diagram should communicate these responsibilities
759	accurately; listing classes alone does not establish a sound design.
760	
761	How did I apply it?
762	
763	    � Combining inheritance with composition. I retained ComboMeal as a subtype of MenuItem that
764	         holds references to two existing menu items. Inheritance expresses that a combo can be used
765	         wherever a menu item is expected; the component references express what it contains. Its price
766	         is 90% of the combined prices and its preparation units are their sum. Chef, Cashier and Menu
767	         needed no Stage 3 changes. I understood this as practical evidence of substitutability and the
768	         Open/Closed Principle: existing consumers handle the extension through their existing contract.
769	         Small setup changes, such as registering the combos in Restaurant, were still necessary.
770	
771	    � Giving the agreed bill one owner. I accepted price locking at order time: the Waiter asks the
772	         customer for its price, applies Happy Hour, and stores the result in Order. A VIP's Burger during
773	         Happy Hour is $15 x 0.9 x 0.8 = $10.80. The Cashier collects the stored amount. My reason for
774	         retaining this approach was temporal consistency: a promotion ending before payment should
775	         not change an already agreed bill. It also keeps the Cashier independent of customer-specific
776	         pricing and promotion timing.
777	
778	    � Choosing the right amount of abstraction. I retained a concrete HappyHour class rather than
779	         adding a PricingPolicy interface. The game has one promotion policy, so I judged that
780	         another interface would add indirection without solving a current variation problem. I
781	         recognised the trade-off: the Waiter depends on that concrete class. If another interchangeable
782	         policy were required, I would reconsider the boundary and extract an abstraction. This taught
783	         me to justify interfaces through actual responsibilities and variation.
784	
785	    � Separating responsibilities and protecting ownership. I chose to address the controller's
786	         accumulated responsibilities in the Stage 3 clean-up. Parsing moved to ManagerCommand, task
787	         execution to StaffActions, and Restaurant became the source of eligible-customer lists for
788	         both control and display. The waiting queue became read-only to callers, and the unused static
789	         order counter was removed. This reduced GameController from 324 to 150 lines and left it
790	         focused on turn sequencing. My acceptance criterion was improved structure without changed
791	         gameplay; the recorded verification includes byte-identical victory, defeat and invalid-input
792	         transcripts before and after refactoring.
793	
794	    � Making extension boundaries verifiable. I required each stage tag to run independently and
795	         used the README comparisons to explain why files changed or stayed unchanged. The checks
796	         also reject customer-type references in GameController, StaffActions and ManagerCommand.
797	         Together with tests of customer behaviour, these provide evidence for the intended separation
798	         of responsibilities. I learned to support an OOP claim with both code structure and observable
799	         behaviour.
800	
801	What challenged me?
802	The hardest part was reconciling the intended design with the implementation while keeping the code,
803	diagrams, README and three tags consistent. A review finding did not automatically determine which
804	artefact should change; I had to consider the responsibility involved and the consequences of moving it.
805	
806	    � Diagrams ahead of the code. The early Stage 2 and 3 diagrams placed
807	         Customer.canFinishEating, Customer.canPay and Restaurant.findOldestEligiblePayer
808	         in the controller. I had to decide whether the code should follow the drawing or whether the
809	         existing placement better protected domain responsibilities.
810	
811	    � Drawing within the tool's limits. Without Graphviz, PlantUML used its built-in Smetana layout.
812	         Multiplicity labels collided near Customer, and ComboMeal inheritance and aggregation lines
813	         overlapped. I needed to improve readability while preserving the intended relationships.
814	
815	    � Representing staged development clearly. The implementation commits were concentrated
816	         into a short period, and later-stage diagram files were already present in stage-1 because the
817	         diagrams were prepared first. Independently runnable tags demonstrate the source boundaries,
818	         but the history communicates the development process less clearly than it could.
819	
820	    � A bug the recorded runs missed. SERVE or COOK without a customer number selected from all
821	         customers who had ordered, rather than those eligible for that task. The recorded runs always
822	         supplied the number, so they did not exercise that path.
823	
824	How did I solve it?
825	
826	    � Resolve disagreements through responsibility. I kept eating and payment eligibility on
827	         Customer, because those rules depend on its status and turn information. I kept payer selection
828	         on Restaurant, because it owns the customer collection. Moving them into the controller
829	         merely to match a draft would increase its knowledge of domain details. I therefore corrected
830	         the diagrams. Member-by-member comparisons against the compiled classes then exposed
831	         missing attributes and operations, which were reconciled for each stage. This separated the
832	         design decision about where a rule belongs from the mechanical check that the diagram
833	         describes it correctly.
834	
835	    � Make diagram compromises explicit. After trying alternative ranks and link placements, I put
836	         multiplicities in link labels and coloured the ComboMeal aggregation orange, documenting the
837	         layout compromise. I subsequently corrected the Table to Customer multiplicity to 0..1 and the
838	         queue notation across the diagrams. I treated readability and semantic accuracy as separate
839	         concerns that both needed attention.
840	
841	    � Verify the stage boundaries. I preserved commit timestamps and focused on making the stages
842	         independently usable: Stage 1 Java source excludes the later customer types and Stage 3
843	         features, while Stage 2 excludes Stage 3 classes. Clean git archive builds and each tag's own
844	         checks provided recorded verification. The README explains the additions and modifications,
845	         including the Stage 3 refactoring.
846	
847	    � Reuse the domain's eligibility rules. I addressed the shortcut bug by having StaffActions use
848	         Restaurant.getCustomersToServe() and getCustomersToCook(). This applies the same
849	         rules to explicit and implicit customer selection. Five additional checks cover the correction,
850	         including the ambiguous case where two orders are READY (983cc8a). The fix reinforced why
851	         eligibility should have one owner instead of being reconstructed separately by each caller.
852	What I would do differently. I would record the rationale for each core decision when making it,
853	including alternatives considered and why I rejected them. I would develop and reconcile each stage's
854	diagram alongside its implementation, with commits that make the progression easier to follow. I would
855	also test commands both with and without arguments and start the written design discussion and video
856	script earlier. This project strengthened my ability to explain and defend an OOP design: identify the
857	owner of a rule, define a useful abstraction, evaluate the cost of a dependency, and verify that an
858	extension preserves existing behaviour.
859	
860	8.3 Ryan
861	What did I learn?
862	
863	I gained a better understanding of how OOP principles can be applied to accommodate real life changing
864	software requirements. I learned inheritance, polymorphism and abstraction support the Open/Closed
865	Principle by allowing new functionality to be introduced without extensively modifying existing classes. I
866	also improved my ability to construct UML class diagrams and represent relationships between classes
867	including inheritance and associations. The project helped me see how they contribute to designing a
868	real system outside of only theory in lectures.
869	
870	How did I apply it?
871	
872	My main contribution was designing parts of Stage 2 and 3 UML class diagrams and explaining how I
873	implemented the SOLID principles.
874	
875	For Stage 2, I focused on introducing VIPCustomer and CriticCustomer as subclasses of the abstract
876	Customer class. This allowed different customer types to have specialized behaviors such as waiting
877	satisfaction penalties, discounts and ordering preferences while following the same general restaurant
878	workflow.
879	
880	For Stage 3, I focused on extending the menu system through ComboMeal which inherits from
881	MenuItem and contains two existing menu items. This design allows combo meals to be treated as
882	menu items withing the existing ordering structure.
883	
884	These designs help me apple lecture content and understand the importance of designing classes that
885	can accommodate new requirements.
886	
887	What challenged me?
888	
889	One of the biggest challenges was determining how to incorporate the new requirements into the
890	existing class structure without creating unnecessary dependencies or modifying too many classes. I
891	initially found it difficult to decide whether new features should be modelled as subclasses or handled
892	by modifying existing classes.
893	
894	Another challenge was constructing and presenting the UML diagrams. As more classes, methods and
895	relationships were introduced, the diagrams became increasingly complicated and difficult to read. I also
896	had limited experience using Google Colab and Mermaid for UML diagrams, which made generating,
897	formatting and exporting the diagrams challenging.
898	
899	How did I solve it?
900	To address the design challenges, I revisited the concepts of inheritance, polymorphism and SOLID
901	principles, particularly OCP and LSP. I examined how customer-specific behaviors could be encapsulated
902	within subclasses rather than implemented using repeated conditional checks in the controller.
903	
904	Similarly, for the Stage 3 menu extension, I used the existing MenuItem abstraction to represent
905	ComboMeal, allowing the design to reuse the ordering structure.
906	
907	For the diagram-related difficulties, I experimented with different rendering and export methods in
908	Google Colab. After encountering issues with diagram scaling and image generation, I exported the
909	diagrams as PNG files instead of relying on screenshots. I also organised the diagrams and design
910	documentation for inclusion in the group's GitHub repository and final report.
911	
912	This experience taught me that designing extensible software requires more than applying inheritance
913	mechanically. It involves understanding each class's responsibility, considering the impact of new
914	requirements on existing components, and making design decisions that maintain clarity and flexibility.
915	
916	8.4 Ye Fei
917	1. My Contribution
918	
919	My main responsibility was designing the Stage 1 UML class diagram. I started by going through the
920	requirements and creating an initial design in Visual Paradigm, including the main classes, their
921	attributes, methods, and relationships.
922	
923	After completing the initial design, I discussed it with my teammates responsible for Stage 2, Stage 3,
924	and Java implementation. Based on their feedback, I made several changes to better support the game
925	rules. For example, I added getOrder() to Customer, introduced servedTurn and readyTurn to handle
926	timing restrictions, and added releaseTableOf(Customer) to Restaurant to support table release after
927	payment.
928	
929	Although I was only responsible for designing the Stage 1 class diagram, I also helped check the
930	consistency of the diagrams across all three stages, particularly their class relationships, methods, and
931	compatibility with the implementation.
932	
933	Besides the UML work, I presented part of our project's design in the recorded presentation and was
934	responsible for editing and combining everyone's recordings into the final video.
935	
936	2. What I Learned
937	
938	Before this project, I mainly saw UML class diagrams as a way to show the structure of a program. While
939	working on the Stage 1 design, I realised that deciding what each class should be responsible for was
940	more challenging than simply identifying classes and methods.
941	
942	One concept that became clearer to me was the Single Responsibility Principle (SRP). I learned that a
943	class should have a clear responsibility rather than handling too many unrelated tasks. This also helped
944	me understand how high cohesion and low coupling can make a program easier to maintain.
945	
946	I also gained a better understanding of encapsulation. Instead of allowing different classes to directly
947	modify each other's attributes, it is better to provide methods that allow objects to manage their own
948	states. Applying these concepts to my own UML design helped me understand them better than simply
949	studying their definitions.
950	3. How I Applied What I Learned
951	When designing the Stage 1 class diagram, I tried to apply SRP by separating responsibilities between
952	different classes. For example, GameController coordinates the game flow, RestaurantCLI handles user
953	interaction, while Waiter, Chef, and Cashier perform their respective tasks. This helped avoid putting too
954	much logic into one class and made the design easier to understand.
955	I also applied encapsulation when revising the diagram. After discussing the initial design with my
956	teammates, I added getOrder() to Customer so other classes could access a customer's order through a
957	method rather than directly accessing its internal attribute. I also added servedTurn and readyTurn to
958	represent the timing restrictions for serving and payment. Another adjustment was adding
959	releaseTableOf(Customer) to Restaurant, so the responsibility for managing tables remained within the
960	restaurant rather than being assigned directly to Cashier.
961	
962	Through these changes, I realised that applying design principles is not just about creating more classes
963	or methods. It is also about deciding where each responsibility should belong and how objects should
964	communicate.
965	4. Challenges and Solutions
966	One challenge was translating the written requirements into a class diagram that could actually support
967	the game. Initially, I focused more on identifying classes and their relationships, so I overlooked some
968	details, especially the timing rules for eating and payment. After my teammates pointed these out, I
969	revisited the requirements and revised the relevant attributes and methods.
970	
971	I also found it difficult to distinguish between Association, Composition, and Dependency. Sometimes
972	two classes interact, but that does not necessarily mean one should own the other. I reviewed the
973	lecture materials and considered how the objects would interact during gameplay before deciding which
974	relationships were more appropriate.
975	
976	Another challenge was that my teammates and I did not always agree on certain class relationships or
977	where methods should belong. In some cases, both approaches seemed reasonable. We discussed the
978	reasoning behind our choices and considered how different designs would affect the system. Thinking
979	about SRP also helped me decide which class should take responsibility for particular operations.
980	
981	Although I did not design the Stage 2 and Stage 3 diagrams, I helped review their consistency with the
982	earlier design. Since later stages introduced new classes and behaviours, checking these changes also
983	helped me understand how an initial design might need to evolve when new requirements are
984	introduced.
985	
986	5. Key Takeaway
987	
988	Overall, this project helped me understand how object-oriented design principles, especially SRP and
989	encapsulation, can guide actual design decisions. I learned that creating a class diagram is not just about
990	drawing classes and connecting them, but also about assigning responsibilities properly and ensuring the
991	design can support the implementation. I also realised that discussing different approaches with
992	teammates and revising the initial design are important parts of developing a better solution.
993	
994	9. AI use and links
995	
996	We used AI tools such as Claude for drafting code, reviewing diagrams against the code, rendering
997	PlantUML and drafting parts of this report. Every member checked and edited what they used, and the
998	game behaviour is verified by GameTests and the recorded runs rather than taken on trust. The full
999	conversations, in order, are in each member's folder:
1000	
1001	    � Li Mu-En
1002	    � Peramudhan
1003	    � Ryan
1004	    � Ye Fei

# Diagrams

The `.puml` files in this folder (`docs/diagrams/src/`) are the sources of truth. The `.pdf` files one level up (`docs/diagrams/`) are generated (vector); do not edit them by hand.

| Diagram | Source | PDF | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stage 1 class | `stage-1-class.puml` | `stage-1-class.pdf` | Redrawn in PlantUML from the Visual Paradigm model `stage-1-class.uml` (kept as the archive). Every class is new. |
| Stage 2 class | `stage-2-class.puml` | `stage-2-class.pdf` | Colour key: green new in Stage 2, yellow changed since Stage 1, blue unchanged. |
| Stage 3 class | `stage-3-class.puml` | `stage-3-class.pdf` | Colour key: green new in Stage 3, yellow changed since Stage 2, blue unchanged. |
| Stage 3 sequence | `stage-3-sequence.puml` | `stage-3-sequence.pdf` | A VIP orders a Burger with Happy Hour on. Turns 1 to 5 are drawn one by one over three pages (setup and turn 1; turns 2 and 3; turns 4 and 5 and the result). The Host is left at WAIT in turns 1 to 3 and seats customer 2 (the base-setting turn-4 arrival) in turn 4; customer 2 is not traced further. |

Plain accessors that no other diagram uses are left out of the class diagrams. Operations that the sequence diagram calls are always listed, and every message in the sequence diagram is an operation declared in the Stage 3 class diagram.

## Drawing conventions

- Three links meet at the top-left corner of `Customer` (`customers`, `waitingQueue` from `Restaurant`, and `occupant` from `Table`), and end labels there overlapped. Their multiplicities at the `Customer` end are written in the link label instead (`customers [0..*]`, `occupant [0..1]`). The same is done for `Customer` to `Order` (`order [0..1]`).
- `occupant` is directed from `Table`, which holds the reference. A table has at most one customer, and a customer has at most one table; waiting and departed customers have none. Both association ends are `0..1`.
- `waitingQueue` is ordered by arrival. The manager may choose any eligible queued customer, so the association does not impose FIFO seating.
- `ComboMeal` is both a `MenuItem` (hollow-triangle line) and made of two `MenuItem` components (orange diamond line, `components [2]`). Both lines join the same two boxes, so the aggregation is coloured and its multiplicity is in the label.

## Regenerating

You need Java, a Chromium-based browser and a PlantUML jar (1.2025.4 was used):

```
node docs/diagrams/src/render.mjs path/to/plantuml.jar
```

This renders every `.puml` in this folder to a vector `.pdf` in `docs/diagrams/`. A diagram that PlantUML splits with `newpage` becomes a multi-page PDF; every page is as wide as the widest page and as tall as its own diagram. Set `CHROME` if the script cannot find your browser. If you need a PNG or SVG for a document, export it from the PDF or run PlantUML directly; those formats are not kept in the repo.

## Using the diagrams in the report

The PDFs are drawn large so that nothing overlaps. At 100% the text is 9 pt, and the page sizes are:

| PDF | Page size (pt) |
|---|---|
| `stage-1-class.pdf` | 1207 x 1460 |
| `stage-2-class.pdf` | 1481 x 1503 |
| `stage-3-class.pdf` | 1562 x 1979 |
| `stage-3-sequence.pdf` | 1792 wide; pages 1704, 1436 and 1484 tall |

Shrunk to A4 landscape width (842 pt) the text drops to about 4 pt, which is too small to read in print. Give each diagram its own full A3 landscape page (about 66%, text about 6 pt), or place it full-width on a rotated A4 page and keep the vector PDF in the repo for zooming. Import the three sequence pages as separate figures rather than as one scaled page.

The class diagrams use PlantUML's built-in Smetana layout, so Graphviz is not needed. Smetana cannot route labelled same-row links, so sideways links carry no mid-link label.

## Keeping diagrams in step with the code

Each diagram must match the code of the stage it describes. The class diagrams were aligned with the Java code at tags `stage-1`, `stage-2` and `stage-3`, and every member listed in a class diagram (name and visibility) was checked against the compiled classes of that tag. Before changing a stage, check again and re-render.

Every attribute, constant, constructor and operation in the code (private helpers included) is listed, together with `Main`, `Money` and the nested `Command` class of the Stage 1 and 2 controllers. A script that compares names, visibility, static flags and parameter and return types against the Java of each tag reported no difference.

=== entity/Customer.java
package restaurantrush.entity;

/**
 * A restaurant guest. Every kind of customer follows the same order and service flow; subclasses
 * differ only in the hooks below (loss per waiting turn, billing, food choice, reaction to being
 * served), so the controller and staff call these on Customer and never check the type.
 * Satisfaction and status are private and change only through the methods of this class.
 * Lifecycle: WAITING -> SEATED -> ORDERED -> SERVED -> READY_TO_PAY -> PAID, or ABANDONED.
 */
public abstract class Customer {
    private static final int MAX_SATISFACTION = 100;

    private final int customerId;
    private int satisfaction = MAX_SATISFACTION;
    private CustomerStatus status = CustomerStatus.WAITING;
    private Order order;
    private int servedTurn;
    private int readyTurn;

    protected Customer(int customerId) {
        this.customerId = customerId;
    }

    public int getId() {
        return customerId;
    }

    public CustomerStatus getStatus() {
        return status;
    }

    public int getSatisfaction() {
        return satisfaction;
    }

    /** Short name of the customer type, for display. */
    public abstract String getTypeLabel();

    /** How messages refer to this customer, for example "Customer #3 (Regular)". */
    public String getDisplayName() {
        return "Customer #" + customerId + " (" + getTypeLabel() + ")";
    }

    /** Satisfaction lost at the end of each turn spent waiting for service. */
    protected abstract int getSatisfactionLoss();

    /** Documented food rule: menu entry number (customerId mod menu size). The Critic overrides it. */
    public MenuItem chooseMenuItem(Menu menu) {
        return menu.getItem(customerId % menu.size());
    }

    /** The bill for a dish at the given list price. The VIP overrides it to apply a discount. */
    public int calculateBillCents(int basePriceCents) {
        return basePriceCents;
    }

    /** Called when the Waiter serves this customer; turnsWaited counts turns since the order was taken. */
    public void onServed(int turnsWaited) {
    }

    /** Only subclasses may move satisfaction directly; the value always stays within 0..100. */
    protected void adjustSatisfaction(int delta) {
        satisfaction = Math.max(0, Math.min(MAX_SATISFACTION, satisfaction + delta));
    }

    /** End-of-turn loss for a customer who has not been served yet; everyone else loses nothing. */
    public void reduceSatisfaction() {
        if (isAwaitingService()) {
            adjustSatisfaction(-getSatisfactionLoss());
        }
    }

    /** Happy Hour: a customer who is still waiting for a table or food regains satisfaction (never above 100). */
    public void recoverSatisfaction(int amount) {
        if (isAwaitingService()) {
            adjustSatisfaction(amount);
        }
    }

    public void assignOrder(Order newOrder) {
        require(CustomerStatus.SEATED);
        order = newOrder;
        status = CustomerStatus.ORDERED;
    }

    public Order getOrder() {
        return order;
    }

    public void markSeated() {
        require(CustomerStatus.WAITING);
        status = CustomerStatus.SEATED;
    }

    public void markServed(int turn) {
        require(CustomerStatus.ORDERED);
        servedTurn = turn;
        status = CustomerStatus.SERVED;
    }

    /** A served customer eats for one full turn: allowed from the turn after the serving turn. */
    public boolean canFinishEating(int turn) {
        return status == CustomerStatus.SERVED && turn > servedTurn;
    }

    public void markReadyToPay(int turn) {
        if (!canFinishEating(turn)) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Customer #" + customerId + " has not finished eating yet");
        }
        readyTurn = turn;
        status = CustomerStatus.READY_TO_PAY;
    }

    /** The Cashier may charge from the turn after the customer became READY_TO_PAY. */
    public boolean canPay(int turn) {
        return status == CustomerStatus.READY_TO_PAY && turn > readyTurn;
    }

    public void markPaid() {
        require(CustomerStatus.READY_TO_PAY);
        status = CustomerStatus.PAID;
    }

    public boolean shouldAbandon() {
        return isAwaitingService() && satisfaction <= 0;
    }

    public void markAbandoned() {
        if (!isAwaitingService()) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Customer #" + customerId + " is " + status + " and cannot abandon");
        }
        status = CustomerStatus.ABANDONED;
    }

    /** True while the customer is waiting for a table, to order, or for food (not yet served). */
    public boolean isAwaitingService() {
        return status == CustomerStatus.WAITING || status == CustomerStatus.SEATED
                || status == CustomerStatus.ORDERED;
    }

    private void require(CustomerStatus expected) {
        if (status != expected) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Customer #" + customerId + " is " + status + ", not " + expected);
        }
    }
}
=== entity/VIPCustomer.java
package restaurantrush.entity;

/** A VIP is more patient (loses 5 per waiting turn) and pays 90% of the list price. */
public class VIPCustomer extends Customer {
    private static final int SATISFACTION_LOSS = 5;
    private static final int PRICE_PERCENT = 90;

    public VIPCustomer(int customerId) {
        super(customerId);
    }

    @Override
    public String getTypeLabel() {
        return "VIP";
    }

    @Override
    protected int getSatisfactionLoss() {
        return SATISFACTION_LOSS;
    }

    /** 10% off, rounded to the nearest cent. */
    @Override
    public int calculateBillCents(int basePriceCents) {
        return (basePriceCents * PRICE_PERCENT + 50) / 100;
    }
}
=== entity/CriticCustomer.java
package restaurantrush.entity;

/**
 * A demanding customer: loses 12 per waiting turn, always orders the most expensive dish, and is
 * further upset (-10) when served more than 2 turns after the order was taken.
 */
public class CriticCustomer extends Customer {
    private static final int SATISFACTION_LOSS = 12;
    private static final int LATE_SERVICE_TURNS = 2;
    private static final int LATE_PENALTY = 10;

    public CriticCustomer(int customerId) {
        super(customerId);
    }

    @Override
    public String getTypeLabel() {
        return "Critic";
    }

    @Override
    protected int getSatisfactionLoss() {
        return SATISFACTION_LOSS;
    }

    @Override
    public MenuItem chooseMenuItem(Menu menu) {
        return menu.getMostExpensive();
    }

    @Override
    public void onServed(int turnsWaited) {
        if (turnsWaited > LATE_SERVICE_TURNS) {
            adjustSatisfaction(-LATE_PENALTY);
        }
    }
}
=== entity/Waiter.java
package restaurantrush.entity;

/**
 * The Waiter seats customers, takes their orders and serves finished dishes. The controller lets
 * the Waiter do at most one of these per turn; each method rejects an impossible request by
 * throwing, before anything is changed.
 */
public class Waiter {

    public void seatCustomer(Customer customer, Table table) {
        if (customer.getStatus() != CustomerStatus.WAITING) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Customer #" + customer.getId() + " is " + customer.getStatus()
                    + "; only a customer in the waiting queue can be seated");
        }
        if (!table.isAvailable()) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Table " + table.getTableId() + " is already occupied");
        }
        table.seat(customer);
        customer.markSeated();
    }

    /**
     * The customer chooses the dish and works out their own bill (a VIP gets a discount); then the
     * Happy Hour discount, if active, is applied on top. The bill is locked in the new Order.
     */
    public Order takeOrder(Customer customer, Menu menu, HappyHour happyHour, int currentTurn) {
        if (customer.getStatus() != CustomerStatus.SEATED) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Customer #" + customer.getId() + " is " + customer.getStatus()
                    + "; only a seated customer can order");
        }
        MenuItem item = customer.chooseMenuItem(menu);
        int billCents = happyHour.applyDiscount(customer.calculateBillCents(item.getPriceCents()));
        Order order = new Order(item, billCents, currentTurn);
        customer.assignOrder(order);
        return order;
    }

    public void serveOrder(Customer customer, int currentTurn) {
        Order order = customer.getOrder();
        if (customer.getStatus() != CustomerStatus.ORDERED || order == null) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Customer #" + customer.getId() + " is " + customer.getStatus()
                    + "; there is no order waiting to be served");
        }
        if (!order.isReady()) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Customer #" + customer.getId() + "'s " + order.getItem().getName()
                    + " is not READY yet (" + order.getStatus() + ", " + order.getCompletedUnits() + "/"
                    + order.getItem().getPreparationUnits() + " units)");
        }
        order.markServed();
        customer.markServed(currentTurn);
        customer.onServed(currentTurn - order.getTurnTaken());
    }
}
=== entity/ComboMeal.java
package restaurantrush.entity;

import java.util.List;

/**
 * A combo is a menu item made of two existing menu items. It costs 90% of their combined price
 * (rounded to the nearest cent) and takes the sum of their preparation units, so the Waiter, Chef
 * and Cashier treat it like any other dish.
 */
public class ComboMeal extends MenuItem {
    private static final int PRICE_PERCENT = 90;

    private final MenuItem first;
    private final MenuItem second;

    public ComboMeal(MenuItem first, MenuItem second) {
        super(first.getName() + " + " + second.getName(),
                ((first.getPriceCents() + second.getPriceCents()) * PRICE_PERCENT + 50) / 100,
                first.getPreparationUnits() + second.getPreparationUnits());
        this.first = first;
        this.second = second;
    }

    public List<MenuItem> getComponents() {
        return List.of(first, second);
    }
}
=== entity/HappyHour.java
package restaurantrush.entity;

/**
 * A pricing policy the manager may switch on once per game, at the start of a turn. It lasts for
 * that turn and the next. While active, orders taken get 20% off and customers still waiting for
 * a table or food recover 15 satisfaction at the start of the turn. It never changes an order
 * that is already placed, because the bill is locked in the Order.
 */
public class HappyHour {
    private static final int DISCOUNT_PERCENT = 20;
    private static final int RECOVERY = 15;
    private static final int DURATION = 2;

    private boolean used;
    private int remainingTurns;

    /** Switches Happy Hour on. Returns false (and changes nothing) if it was already used this game. */
    public boolean activate() {
        if (used) {
            return false;
        }
        used = true;
        remainingTurns = DURATION;
        return true;
    }

    public boolean isActive() {
        return remainingTurns > 0;
    }

    /** True until the manager switches Happy Hour on; it cannot be used a second time. */
    public boolean isAvailable() {
        return !used;
    }

    public int getRemainingTurns() {
        return remainingTurns;
    }

    /** The bill after the Happy Hour discount (rounded to the nearest cent), or unchanged when it is not active. */
    public int applyDiscount(int cents) {
        if (!isActive()) {
            return cents;
        }
        return (cents * (100 - DISCOUNT_PERCENT) + 50) / 100;
    }

    /** Satisfaction a waiting customer recovers at the start of an active turn; 0 when not active. */
    public int getRecoveryAmount() {
        return isActive() ? RECOVERY : 0;
    }

    /** Called once at the end of every turn. */
    public void endTurn() {
        if (remainingTurns > 0) {
            remainingTurns--;
        }
    }
}
=== entity/Host.java
package restaurantrush.entity;

/**
 * The Host seats at most one queued customer per turn, in addition to whatever the Waiter does
 * (the Waiter can still seat customers too). The controller allows one Host task per turn.
 */
public class Host {

    /** Rejects an occupied table or a customer who is not in the waiting queue, before changing anything. */
    public void seatCustomer(Customer customer, Table table) {
        if (!table.isAvailable()) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Table " + table.getTableId() + " is already occupied");
        }
        customer.markSeated();
        table.seat(customer);
    }
}

### Tool result: toolu_01XjH2z8WX8VnJpzR8pixc6T

package restaurantrush.control;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Optional;
import java.util.function.Function;
import java.util.function.Supplier;

import restaurantrush.boundary.RestaurantCLI;
import restaurantrush.entity.Customer;
import restaurantrush.entity.HappyHour;
import restaurantrush.entity.Money;
import restaurantrush.entity.Restaurant;

/**
 * Runs the game: 18 turns of six steps each (arrivals, manager choices, kitchen and serving,
 * eating and payment, waiting and abandonment, result). It owns the turn counter and the order of
 * the steps. Reading a choice is the CLI's job, carrying it out is StaffActions' job and deciding
 * whether it is legal is the domain objects' job; the controller only sequences them.
 */
public class GameController {
    private static final int MAX_TURNS = 18;

    private final Restaurant restaurant;
    private final ArrivalSchedule schedule;
    private final StaffActions staff;
    private int currentTurn;
    private final int maxTurns;
    private final RestaurantCLI cli;

    public GameController(RestaurantCLI cli) {
        this.cli = cli;
        this.restaurant = new Restaurant();
        this.schedule = new ArrivalSchedule();
        this.staff = new StaffActions(restaurant);
        this.maxTurns = MAX_TURNS;
    }

    /** Plays all turns (there is no early ending), then reports victory or defeat. */
    public void startGame() {
        cli.displayIntro(maxTurns);
        while (currentTurn < maxTurns) {
            currentTurn++;
            executeTurn();
        }
        cli.displayResult(restaurant.hasMetObjective(), restaurant, currentTurn);
    }

    private void executeTurn() {
        processScheduledArrival();
        cli.displayTurnStatus(restaurant, currentTurn, maxTurns);
        processHappyHourActivation();
        applyHappyHourRecovery();
        coordinateHostAction();
        coordinateStaffActions();
        coordinatePaymentProcessing();
        processEndOfTurnUpdates();
    }

    /** Step 1: the schedule decides who arrives at the start of turns 1, 4, 7, 10, 13 and 16. */
    private void processScheduledArrival() {
        if (schedule.isArrivalTurn(currentTurn)) {
            restaurant.registerArrival(schedule.createArrival(currentTurn));
        }
    }

    /** Start of turn: while Happy Hour is unused the manager may switch it on (once per game). */
    private void processHappyHourActivation() {
        HappyHour happyHour = restaurant.getHappyHour();
        if (happyHour.isAvailable() && cli.readHappyHourChoice() && happyHour.activate()) {
            cli.displayMessage("Happy Hour is ON: orders taken now get a discount and waiting customers recover "
                    + "satisfaction. Turns left: " + happyHour.getRemainingTurns() + ".");
        }
    }

    /** Start of an active Happy Hour turn: customers still waiting for a table or food recover satisfaction. */
    private void applyHappyHourRecovery() {
        HappyHour happyHour = restaurant.getHappyHour();
        if (!happyHour.isActive()) {
            return;
        }
        int amount = happyHour.getRecoveryAmount();
        List<String> changes = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Customer customer : restaurant.getCustomers()) {
            if (customer.isAwaitingService()) {
                int before = customer.getSatisfaction();
                customer.recoverSatisfaction(amount);
                changes.add("#" + customer.getId() + " " + before + " -> " + customer.getSatisfaction());
            }
        }
        cli.displayMessage("Happy Hour (" + happyHour.getRemainingTurns() + " turn(s) left including this one): "
                + (changes.isEmpty() ? "nobody is waiting." : "waiting customers recover " + String.join(", ", changes) + "."));
    }

    /** Step 2 (Host): at most one queued customer is seated, on top of the Waiter's own task. */
    private void coordinateHostAction() {
        chooseUntilAccepted(cli::readHostChoice, staff::host);
    }

    /** Steps 2 and 3: the Waiter acts first, then the Chef. A rejected choice does not use up the task. */
    private void coordinateStaffActions() {
        chooseUntilAccepted(cli::readWaiterChoice, command -> staff.waiter(command, currentTurn));
        chooseUntilAccepted(cli::readChefChoice, staff::chef);
    }

    private void chooseUntilAccepted(Supplier<String> reader, Function<ManagerCommand, Optional<String>> task) {
        while (true) {
            String choice = reader.get();
            try {
                task.apply(ManagerCommand.parse(choice)).ifPresent(cli::displayMessage);
                return;
            } catch (IllegalArgumentException | IllegalStateException rejected) {
                cli.displayError(rejected.getMessage());
            }
        }
    }

    /** Step 4: served customers finish eating, then the Cashier charges at most one READY_TO_PAY customer. */
    private void coordinatePaymentProcessing() {
        for (Customer customer : restaurant.getCustomers()) {
            if (customer.canFinishEating(currentTurn)) {
                customer.markReadyToPay(currentTurn);
                cli.displayMessage(customer.getDisplayName() + " finishes eating and is ready to pay.");
            }
        }
        Customer payer = restaurant.findOldestEligiblePayer(currentTurn);
        if (payer != null) {
            int bill = payer.getOrder().getBillCents();
            restaurant.getCashier().processPayment(payer, restaurant);
            cli.displayMessage("Cashier collects " + Money.format(bill) + " from " + payer.getDisplayName()
                    + " (satisfaction " + payer.getSatisfaction() + "). The table is free again.");
        }
    }

    /** Step 5: customers who are still waiting lose satisfaction; at 0 they leave without paying. */
    private void processEndOfTurnUpdates() {
        for (Customer customer : restaurant.getCustomers()) {
            if (!customer.isAwaitingService()) {
                continue;
            }
            customer.reduceSatisfaction();
            if (customer.shouldAbandon()) {
                restaurant.handleAbandonment(customer);
                cli.displayMessage(customer.getDisplayName()
                        + " runs out of patience and leaves without paying. Any unfinished order is cancelled.");
            }
        }
        restaurant.getHappyHour().endTurn();
    }
}

// A light fill for a color on light themes, a translucent one on dark themes.
export function tintOf(theme, c) {
  const base = colorOf(theme, c, 'accent');
  if (base === 'none') return 'none';
  if (theme.dark) return withAlpha(base, theme.tintStrength);
  return mixColor(theme.surface, base, theme.tintStrength);
}

export function fontString(theme, role = 'body', size = 48, weight) {
  const f = theme.fonts[role] || theme.fonts.body;
  const w = weight ?? f.weight;
  const fallback = role === 'mono' ? 'monospace' : 'sans-serif';
  return `${w} ${Math.round(size * 100) / 100}px "${f.family}", "Noto Sans Devanagari", "Noto Sans JP", "Noto Sans SC", ${fallback}`;
}

# Restaurant Rush

> One "##" block per scene. The id after "##" must match a function in scenes.js.
> Write narration the way you would say it. [#name] marks a moment you can
> animate on, [pause 0.5] adds silence, {API|A P I} shows "API" but says "A P I".

## hook
Most people never think about what happens [#click] after they click a link.

## steps
It takes three steps. First, [#one] your browser finds the server. Then [#two] it asks for the page. Finally, [#three] it draws what comes back.

## outro {hold=1.5}
Three steps, [#fast] in less time than a blink.
// One function per scene in script.md. Each function draws one frame of its
// scene, and s.t is the time in seconds since the scene started. Give elements
// an `at` time (seconds, a spoken word or "#marker") and the look animates
// them in. The full scene API is in AGENTS.md in the explainroo folder.

export default {
  hook(s) {
    s.title('Restaurant Rush', { y: s.safe.y + s.safe.h * 0.38 });
    s.icon('mouse-pointer-click', { y: s.safe.y + s.safe.h * 0.7, size: 130, at: '#click', color: 'accent' });
  },

  steps(s) {
    // s.safe is the part of the frame that is free for content. Wide videos
    // show the steps side by side, tall ones stack them.
    const tall = s.H > s.W;
    s.text('Three steps', { font: 'display', size: 76, y: s.safe.y + 50, at: 0 });
    const spots = tall
      ? s.col(3, { y: s.safe.y + s.safe.h * 0.6, height: s.safe.h * 0.66 }).map((y) => ({ x: s.cx, y }))
      : s.row(3, { width: Math.min(1180, s.safe.w - 260) }).map((x) => ({ x, y: s.cy }));
    s.box('Find the server', { id: 'find', ...spots[0], icon: 'search', color: 'blue', at: '#one' });
    s.box('Ask for the page', { id: 'ask', ...spots[1], icon: 'send', color: 'green', at: '#two' });
    s.box('Draw it', { id: 'draw', ...spots[2], icon: 'paintbrush', color: 'purple', at: '#three' });
    s.arrow('find', 'ask', { at: s.cue('Then') - 0.4 });
    s.arrow('ask', 'draw', { at: s.cue('Finally') - 0.4 });
  },

  outro(s) {
    s.number(3, { suffix: ' steps', y: s.safe.y + s.safe.h * 0.42, at: 0.2 });
    s.subtitle('in less time than a blink', { at: '#fast', y: s.safe.y + s.safe.h * 0.62 });
  },

  "file_path": "C:\\Users\\natha\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\12ca5763a4c52cfd\\03a4b28b-05fd-4e94-b641-00d19448ccad\\scratchpad\\explainroo\\videos\\restaurant-rush\\script.md",
  "content": "# Restaurant Rush\n\n> Group presentation video for the SC2002 Restaurant Rush project.\n> Lines starting with \">\" are notes and are never spoken.\n> \"Presenter\" notes say which member's part each section follows in the report,\n> so a member can record that part in their own voice if the group prefers.\n\n## title {chapter=\"Game overview and the three stages\"}\n> Presenter: Li Mu-En\nThis is Restaurant Rush, the {SC2002|S C two zero zero two} group project from lab group {SCEC|S C E C}, group five. [#java] It is a turn based restaurant game for the command line, written in Java.\n\n## manager\nYou play the manager of a small restaurant. [#staff] You have one Waiter, one Chef and one Cashier, and in Stage three a Host as well. [#limit] The Waiter, Chef and Host can each do only one task per turn, [#choose] so you decide which customer or order comes first.\n\n## base\nThe base setting is fixed. The game lasts eighteen turns, with two tables. [#arrivals] One customer arrives at the start of turns 1, 4, 7, 10, 13 and 16. To win, [#paid] you need at least four paid customers, [#rev] at least forty five dollars in revenue, [#sat] and an average satisfaction of at least sixty among the customers who paid.\n\n## turn\nEvery turn runs six steps in order. [#s1] Arrivals, [#s2] manager choices, [#s3] kitchen and serving, [#s4] eating and payment, [#s5] waiting and abandonment, [#s6] and the result after turn eighteen. [#later] The Waiter acts before the Chef, so a dish that finishes cooking is served on the next turn at the earliest. Eating and payment also happen on later turns.\n\n## stages\nWe built the game in three stages. Each stage is a tagged commit that builds and runs on its own. [#s1] Stage one is the basic game, with Regular customers, the menu, and the Waiter, Chef and Cashier. [#s2] Stage two adds {VIP|V I P} and Critic customers. [#s3] Stage three adds the Host, combo meals and Happy Hour.\n\n## launch {chapter=\"Gameplay demonstration\"}\n> Presenter: Li Mu-En\nHere is the game launching from the submitted project. [#build] From the repository root we compile with {javac|java C}, [#run] and start the game with java. It needs only Java seventeen and no network. [#screen] The screen shows the turn, revenue, average satisfaction, the queue, the tables, every customer, and the tasks each staff member can do.\n\n## choice\nNow a real decision, from turn thirteen of our victory run. [#screen] The Critic's Salad and Pasta combo is ready, and customer five has just arrived. [#host] The Host seats customer five, so the Waiter has two jobs waiting, serving the Critic or taking customer five's order. [#serve] The Waiter can do only one, so we serve the Critic first. [#cards] The Critic then pays with satisfaction fifty four. If we take the order first, the Critic waits another turn and pays with forty two, and the average falls from eighty one point two to seventy eight point eight.\n\n## invalid\nImpossible choices are refused. [#rej] On turn six we ask the Waiter to serve customer three, who does not exist. The game explains why, and asks again. [#kept] The Waiter's task is not used up, so we serve customer two instead.\n\n## lifecycle\nHere is one dish going from ready to served to paid. [#cook] On turn one the Chef finishes the {VIP|V I P}'s Burger and it becomes ready. [#serve] The Waiter has already acted, so the Burger is served on turn two. [#eat] On turn three the customer finishes eating and is ready to pay. [#paid] On turn four the Cashier collects ten dollars and eighty cents, and the table is free again.\n\n## customers\nStage two adds customer behaviour. [#regular] A Regular loses eight satisfaction for each turn spent waiting. [#vip] A {VIP|V I P} loses five, and pays ninety percent of the price, so a Burger costs thirteen dollars fifty. [#critic] A Critic loses twelve, and always orders the most expensive dish. [#late] A Critic also loses ten more if served more than two turns after ordering.\n\n## host\n> Presenter: Li Mu-En\nStage three adds the Host. [#seat] On turn one the Host seats the {VIP|V I P} at table one. [#order] In the same turn, the Waiter takes the order, because the Host works independently of the Waiter. [#chef] The Chef can then start cooking it straight away.\n\n## combo\nA combo meal is made from two existing dishes. [#rule] Its price is ninety percent of the two prices added together, and its preparation units are added too. [#c1] Salad and Burger costs twenty one dollars sixty, and takes two units. [#c2] Salad and Pasta costs twenty four dollars thirty, and takes three units.\n\n## happy\nHappy Hour is a pricing policy that the manager can switch on once per game. [#on] We switch it on at the start of turn one. [#lasts] It lasts for that turn and the next, and the screen counts the turns left. [#disc] Orders taken during it get twenty percent off, after the {VIP|V I P} discount, [#locked] so the Burger is fifteen times point nine times point eight, which is ten dollars eighty. [#recover] Waiting customers also recover fifteen satisfaction. [#used] Afterwards the screen shows Happy Hour as used.\n\n## victory\nBoth endings can be reproduced from recorded inputs. [#cmd] This command replays our victory run. [#win] After turn eighteen, five customers have paid, revenue is eighty three dollars seventy, and average satisfaction is eighty one point two. That meets all three targets. [#sixth] The sixth customer arrives on turn sixteen and cannot finish paying in time, so five is the most that can pay.\n\n## defeat\nThe defeat run switches Happy Hour on, serves the {VIP|V I P}, and then only waits. [#lose] One customer pays ten dollars eighty, with full satisfaction, but that is far below four paid customers and forty five dollars. [#leave] Two customers leave without paying, and a customer who leaves never pays. A happy customer is not enough, because all three targets must be met.\n\n## stage1class {chapter=\"Class diagrams and stage changes\"}\n> Presenter: Ye Fei\nNow the design. This is the Stage one class diagram. [#layers] We split the classes into boundary, control and entity layers. The {RestaurantCLI|Restaurant C L I} only shows the screen and reads choices, and the Game Controller runs the turns. [#restaurant] The Restaurant owns its tables and staff by composition, and holds customers by aggregation. [#customer] Customer keeps its status private, and Order only changes status through guarded methods, so an illegal move throws.\n\n## evolution\nThe same diagram style is used at every stage, so they can be compared. [#s1] Stage one is all blue, because every class is new. [#s2] In Stage two, green classes are new and yellow classes changed. [#s3] In Stage three, the Host, Happy Hour, Combo Meal and two new control classes are green.\n\n## stage2class\n> Presenter: Ryan\nIn Stage two, Customer became abstract. [#sub] Regular, {VIP|V I P} and Critic customers extend it, and override only what differs, the loss per turn, the bill, the food choice and the reaction to service. [#poly] The Waiter and the controller call these methods on a Customer, and never check the type. [#same] Every customer still follows the same order and service flow.\n\n## stage2diff\n> Presenter: Li Mu-En\nAt the stage one tag, the source has no customer subclasses and no later features. [#diff] The compare to stage two shows twelve source files. [#new] Five are new, [#mod] and seven are modified. [#order] Order now stores the locked bill, because the {VIP|V I P} discount means the price is no longer the list price. [#waiter] The Waiter asks the customer for the dish and the bill, which is the one place where type specific behaviour is dispatched. [#same] Chef, Table, Menu Item, the enums and Money are unchanged, and nothing was deleted.\n\n## stage3class\n> Presenter: Ryan\nStage three adds a combo meal, Happy Hour and the Host. [#combo] Combo Meal extends Menu Item, and also holds two existing menu items, so it is a real combination built by composition. The Waiter, Chef, Cashier and Menu handle it without any change. [#final] Menu Item only lost the word final. [#hh] Happy Hour is a policy class. The Waiter applies it after the customer's own price, and the result is locked in the Order.\n\n## stage3diff\n> Presenter: Li Mu-En\nBetween stage two and stage three, five files are new and eight are modified. [#cleanup] After the features worked, we made one separate clean-up commit. It moved command parsing into Manager Command, and task execution into Staff Actions. [#lines] That cut the Game Controller from three hundred twenty four lines to one hundred fifty. [#same] The victory run, the defeat run and an invalid input session print identical transcripts before and after, so no rule changed.\n\n## tests\nEvery tag builds from a clean checkout and passes its own checks. [#t1] Stage one passes fifty five, [#t2] stage two passes one hundred and eight, [#t3] and stage three passes one hundred and ninety four. [#bug] Review also found a real gap that the recorded runs missed. A bare serve or cook command chose from the wrong customers, because the runs always typed a number. We fixed it with the same lists the screen shows, and added five checks.\n\n## principles\n> Presenter: Ryan\nHere is how the design principles show up in the code. [#srp] For single responsibility, the controller only sequences the turn, Manager Command reads a choice, and Staff Actions carries it out. [#ocp] For open and closed, Stage two added three customer types without touching the turn logic, and the combo needed no change in the Chef, Cashier, Menu or Order. [#lsp] For substitution, any customer subclass works where a Customer is expected, and a test puts a three unit combo into an ordinary order. [#dep] The {CLI|C L I} no longer depends on the controller, and a test checks that its source never mentions it. [#noint] We did not add interfaces only to name a principle.\n\n## tradeoffs\n> Presenter: Li Mu-En\nWe also weighed alternatives. [#hh] Happy Hour is a concrete class, because there is only one policy. A pricing policy interface would add indirection without a second variant, and we would extract one if a second promotion appeared. [#lock] The bill is locked in the Order, so a promotion that ends before payment cannot change an agreed price, and the Cashier knows nothing about promotions. [#combo] A combo is a Menu Item holding two items, instead of a plain item with a hard coded price, which would not be a real combination. [#strategy] Behaviour lives in customer subclasses instead of strategy objects. That is simpler, but a mixed type such as a {VIP|V I P} critic would need a new subclass.\n\n## seqcase {chapter=\"Sequence diagram\"}\n> Presenter: Peramudhan\nFor the sequence diagram we chose a Stage three use case. [#vip] A {VIP|V I P} orders a Burger while Happy Hour is on. [#start] It starts on turn one, with an empty restaurant, two free tables, and Happy Hour unused. [#end] It ends on turn five, when the customer pays ten dollars eighty with satisfaction ninety five. [#decision] The manager's decision is whether to switch Happy Hour on. Without it, the bill is thirteen dollars fifty and the satisfaction at payment is ninety.\n\n## seqturn1\nThe lifelines include the manager, the {CLI|C L I} as the boundary, the controller, Staff Actions and Manager Command, and the domain objects. [#arr] In turn one, the controller asks the schedule for an arrival, and the {VIP|V I P} joins the waiting queue. [#alt] An alt fragment shows the manager's yes or no choice for Happy Hour, and yes activates it with two turns remaining. [#seat] The Host waits, so the Waiter seats the {VIP|V I P}, and the Restaurant removes the customer from the queue only after the Waiter accepts. [#loss] At the end of the turn, reduce satisfaction is called on the customer, and the {VIP|V I P} version loses five.\n\n## seqturn2\nIn turn two, Happy Hour gives waiting customers fifteen satisfaction back. [#take] Then the Waiter takes the order. The customer chooses the Burger, calculate bill cents returns thirteen fifty, and apply discount makes it ten eighty. [#lock] A new Order is created with that bill, and it has no setter, so the price stays locked. [#chef] The Chef advances the order, and it becomes ready. The Waiter has already acted this turn, so serving waits until turn three.\n\n## seqturn3\nIn turn three, the Waiter serves inside a loop that repeats until a choice is accepted. [#alt] The alt fragment shows the guard. A ready order is served. [#bad] Otherwise an exception is thrown, the error is shown, and the manager chooses again without losing the task.\n\n## seqpay\nIn turn four, the Host seats the next customer, and the {VIP|V I P} finishes eating and becomes ready to pay. [#cant] The check can pay is false in that turn, so the Cashier does not charge yet. [#cash] In turn five, the Cashier reads the locked bill from the Order, the Restaurant records the revenue, both statuses become paid, and the table is released. [#end] After turn eighteen, has met objective decides victory or defeat, in a final alt.\n\n## seqwhy\nThese messages implement the use case through two decisions. [#poly] The controller never checks the customer type, because the discount and the loss come from overridden methods. [#once] The bill is worked out once, in the Waiter, so the Cashier only reads it. [#run] The recorded run is faster, because the Host seats the {VIP|V I P} while the Waiter takes the order, so payment happens on turn four.\n\n## team {chapter=\"Individual contributions and key learning\"}\nHere is how we shared the work. [#mu] Li Mu-En built the repository, the stage tags and the Java implementation, and reworked the diagrams. [#peram] Peramudhan drafted the Stage three sequence diagram. [#ryan] Ryan designed the Stage two and Stage three class diagrams, and wrote the principles discussion. [#yefei] Ye Fei designed the Stage one class diagram, and reviewed the diagrams for consistency across the stages.\n\n## learnmuen\n> Presenter: Li Mu-En\nLi Mu-En learned to ask which object should own each rule. Keeping the turn guards next to the state they protect, in Customer and Order, means an invalid command cannot half change the game. [#hard] The hardest part was keeping the code, the diagrams, the {README|read me} and the three tags consistent.\n\n## learnperam\n> Presenter: Peramudhan\nPeramudhan learned that each arrow in a sequence diagram states which object owns an action. Drawing the {VIP|V I P} bill showed how polymorphism works in practice. [#hard] The diagram had to be redrawn after the clean-up changed the code, so a sequence diagram should be finished once the code is stable.\n\n## learnryan\n> Presenter: Ryan\nRyan learned that inheritance, polymorphism and abstraction support the open and closed principle. [#hard] The hardest part was deciding between adding a subclass and changing an existing class, and keeping the diagrams readable as they grew.\n\n## learnyefei\n> Presenter: Ye Fei\nYe Fei learned how single responsibility and encapsulation decide where each operation belongs. After feedback, the Stage one diagram gained get order, served turn, ready turn and release table of. [#hard] Telling association, composition and dependency apart was the hardest part.\n\n## closing {hold=1.8}\nWe used AI to help draft code and review diagrams, and every member checked the result. The full conversations, in order, are in each member's folder in the repository. [#repo] Everything is on GitHub, with the stage tags and the compare links. [#end] In short, Restaurant Rush is three runnable stages, and each stage extends the one before it, with every change explained.\n"
}
~~~

    let fx = 0;
    let fy = 0;
    if (o.float) {

      fx = w.x;
      fy = w.y;
    }

    if (o.rotate) ctx.rotate((o.rotate * Math.PI) / 180);
    const sc = (o.scale ?? 1) * tr.s;
    if (sc !== 1) ctx.scale(sc, sc);
    ctx.globalAlpha *= tr.a * (o.opacity ?? 1);
    this._lastTr = tr;
    return tr;

  _drawProgress(life) {
    if (life.enter !== 'draw') return { stroke: 1, fill: 1, text: 1 };
    const p = life.p;
    return { stroke: ease.inOut(clamp(p / 0.78)), fill: clamp((p - 0.42) / 0.58), text: clamp((p - 0.35) / 0.45) };
  }

  // Current transform relative to the render scale: local -> design pixels.
  _matrix() {
    const m = this.ctx.getTransform();
    const s = this.engine.scale;
    return new DOMMatrix([m.a / s, m.b / s, m.c / s, m.d / s, m.e / s, m.f / s]);
  }

  _register(id, x, y, w, h, shape = 'rect') {
    const m = this._matrix();
    const pts = [[x - w / 2, y - h / 2], [x + w / 2, y - h / 2], [x + w / 2, y + h / 2], [x - w / 2, y + h / 2]].map(([px, py]) => m.transformPoint(new DOMPoint(px, py)));
    const xs = pts.map((p) => p.x);
    const ys = pts.map((p) => p.y);
    const box = { x: (Math.min(...xs) + Math.max(...xs)) / 2, y: (Math.min(...ys) + Math.max(...ys)) / 2, w: Math.max(...xs) - Math.min(...xs), h: Math.max(...ys) - Math.min(...ys), shape };
    if (id !== undefined && id !== null) this._ids.set(String(id), box);
    // While the camera is planned, the next _life call gives this box its
    // timing.
    if (this.engine.layoutLog) this._pendingBox = box;
    return box;
  }
